#168. From supermarket fridges to passenger flights, a wave of threats is testing Europe's defenses

Cyberattacks, bomb threats against passenger flights, civil unrest and suspected acts of sabotage across Europe have raised questions about the continent's resilience — and how prepared it is to defend itself against modern security threats.

Hackers remotely manipulated refrigeration systems at supermarkets across Europe last week, forcing retailers to discard spoiled food and exposing vulnerabilities in everyday infrastructure.

French blood-bank refrigeration systems were subsequently targeted in what appeared to be a similar incident, while a wave of suspected hoax bomb threats disrupted dozens of flights across the continent, forcing diversions and, in some cases, fighter jet escorts.

The week culminated with bomb threats targeting European news outlets, including the U.K.'s Sky News and Irish public broadcaster RTÉ.

The incidents come against a backdrop of heightened geopolitical tensions and domestic unrest across parts of Europe, with civilian infrastructure potentially becoming an increasingly attractive target for hostile actors.

While authorities have not established who was behind the attacks or whether they were connected, defense and security experts warn that disruption to everyday services can have consequences far beyond the immediate economic damage.

If Russia were responsible, such attacks could be intended to demonstrate the costs of Europe's continued support for Ukraine, said Sidharth Kaushal, a research fellow at the Royal United Services Institute, or RUSI.

"I think it's more about cost imposition," Kaushal told CNBC. "Convincing the European political leadership that the costs of a war can come home to them."

Russia denied any involvement. "These are stupid accusations. They are baseless," Kremlin spokesperson Dmitry Peskov told CNBC via emailed comments.

On Monday, supermarkets in Belgium, Luxembourg, Sweden and the Netherlands found that refrigeration systems had been set to as much as 50 degrees Celsius, or 122 degrees Fahrenheit, affecting retailers including Ahold Delhaize, IKEA, Colruyt Group and Carrefour.

It followed an October cyberattack that targeted hundreds of French supermarkets, causing fires, fridges to overheat and food to spoil. The incidents highlighted vulnerabilities in food-supply infrastructure.

They also highlight the role of private companies in national resilience, as internet-connected equipment can be exploited to cause physical disruption.

Fredrik Blix, cybersecurity expert at Visente and associate professor at Stockholm University, told CNBC that the supermarket attacks required very little technical sophistication since vulnerable refrigeration control systems were connected directly to the public internet, allowing potential attackers to find and exploit them.

"These attacks, they don't really require any specific advanced knowledge to carry out," he said, adding that some systems were protected only by default passwords or easily guessed credentials.

"This is why this is so cheap to do," Blix said, warning that the publicity surrounding the incidents could encourage copycat attacks. "Anyone can do it, and it does not require any resources from a country or anything like that."

Cyberattacks have become more common over the past decade as societies have become more digital and vulnerable systems have become easier to access, Blix said. However, he noted that mounting geopolitical tensions have also made European countries more alert to potential hybrid warfare threats, leading to scrutiny by both governments and media.

While Blix acknowledged the possibility of a state or state-backed actor being behind the attacks, he said the more likely scenario is someone testing the boundaries and "having some fun."

Kaushal said the incidents should be viewed within the larger context of suspected sabotage and hybrid threats across Europe, including damage to undersea cables in the Baltic Sea.

"If this was an event in isolation, one might say 'okay, we should view it on its own merits,' but when you view it in the backdrop of all of that, it becomes less clear," he said, also citing death threats against European defense executives and a bomb placed on a DHL aircraft in 2024.

European governments are contending with greater uncertainty over U.S. security guarantees after decades of underinvestment in defense, while facing a rise in unconventional but potentially devastating threats.

Hybrid warfare combines tactics such as cyberattacks, sabotage and disinformation to destabilize adversaries without triggering open military conflict.

Countries' abilities to withstand hybrid threats vary substantially across the continent, according to Kaushal. While Finland and Sweden have well-developed civilian defense programs reflecting their Cold War experience, much of Europe's infrastructure was built around economic efficiency rather than defense needs.

"As one goes further west in Europe, the level of preparation, both against hybrid attacks, but also just simple missile attacks of the kind that we've seen in Ukraine, is far less great," he said.

"Every attack generates a bit of resilience," he said, citing NATO's increased focus on threats to underwater cables and gas pipelines after the 2022 Nord Stream sabotage.

Oana Popescu-Zamfir, a senior research fellow specializing in European security at RUSI, said the recent incidents showed Europe had lagged in "preparing for something that we have known would be coming."

"We've been slow in adjusting to a new reality," she said. "We still tend to conceptually divide between peace and wartime, and that is no longer the case because we're not at war, but we're also not in peace time."

She said Europe was likely to face sustained hybrid attacks designed to achieve military objectives without conventional military force.

Popescu-Zamfir said Russia could draw on networks of proxies in Europe, some directly controlled and others more loosely coordinated, making responsibility difficult to trace directly to the Kremlin.

She told CNBC that "the damage that can be done is huge" in targeted attacks on internet-connected systems, citing the potential disruption of blood reserves.

The attacks are not a "piece of cake for Russia to carry out, but they're also not rocket science," she said, adding that they "can do tremendous damage in concrete terms and in terms of the psychological impact on our societies and the feeling that we are unprotected."

Blix said companies can take relatively straightforward steps to reduce their exposure.

"These are basic cyber hygiene things... You should not be allowed to connect industrial control systems and this kind of operational technology directly online without any proper security around it," he said.

Responsibility is split among product manufacturers, maintenance providers and grocery chains, making accountability unclear as each points to the others.

Blix said maintenance companies sometimes prioritize convenience by enabling remote access despite manufacturers' security guidance, while retailers may fail to enforce adequate cybersecurity standards among suppliers.

"What worries me is that we don't really do anything," Blix said, adding that "we are shuffling a lot of papers" and "doing a lot of risk assessments" but failing to take meaningful action.

Europe has also seen widespread unrest in recent months, adding political pressure as households grapple with high living costs.

Domestic political and economic tensions can also give foreign adversaries opportunities to deepen divisions, said Alexander Anderson, president and director of policy at the Centre for International Security and Economic Strategy.

He told CNBC that Russian and potentially Chinese influence operations may work to amplify those divisions.

"But we should be careful not to dismiss legitimate domestic grievances as the product of foreign interference," he said. "Doing so risks misunderstanding both the causes of political instability and the methods through which hostile actors seek to exploit it."

Weeks of nationwide protests, riots and strikes have increased pressure on the French government, complicating efforts to rein in public finances and deepening existing political divisions.

In Spain, demonstrations and a parliamentary impasse over the housing crisis led Prime Minister Pedro Sanchez to call a snap election. The vote is scheduled for Nov. 29. The far-right Vox party is expected to make gains, putting Sanchez's left-leaning government at risk of losing power.

Germany, Italy, Portugal and Albania have also seen unrest over rising living costs in recent weeks.

On Friday, protesters in Amsterdam tried to enter a Microsoft data center, Reuters reported.

Meanwhile, elevated energy prices, persistent inflation, sluggish growth and large budget deficits are pushing up borrowing costs and leaving governments with less room to support struggling households.

#167. Welsh aviation firm AerFin bought in £475m deal

The company said it was beginning a "new chapter"

A Newport aviation company, that has grown into a global supplier of aircraft engines, parts and maintenance services, has been bought in a deal worth about Â£475m.

Japanese firm ORIX Aviation has agreed to acquire AerFin, which employs more than 230 people and operates from sites in the UK and around the world.

Founded by Bob James, AerFin has developed from a south Wales business into a global aviation aftermarket specialist.

Announcing the takeover, AerFin described it as the start of a new chapter for the company.

The firm - which was majority owned by a private equity company prior to the sale - buys, sells, leases and repairs aircraft, engines and components.

In 2025, the company moved into a new purpose-built, 116,000 sq ft facility, external at Indurent Park in Newport.

There are no plans to move its headquarters from Newport.

ORIX Aviation is the aircraft leasing arm of Japanese financial services giant the ORIX Group.

Simon Goodson, AerFin's chief executive, said the deal would help grow the business

Chief executive Simon Goodson called the acquisition a "defining moment" in the company's history.

"I am delighted that AerFin is joining the ORIX Group, a business that shares our values and belief in trusted partnerships, flexible solutions and finding the way ahead for our customers," he said.

He said support from the wider ORIX Group would provide "the confidence and platform" to grow the aviation business.

"This is a defining moment in AerFin's story and the beginning of an exciting new chapter," said Goodson.

Aerfin helps airlines and aviation companies source parts and manage assets

AerFin has built its existing business around extending the life of aircraft and engines through trading, leasing, repair and reuse.

It helps airlines and aviation companies source parts and manage assets. The company claims to serve more than 600 organisations.

AerFin moved into a new purpose-built facility in Newport last year

For ORIX Aviation, the acquisition expands its activities beyond aircraft ownership and leasing, into aftermarket services and end-of-life aircraft management.

The company said the combination would strengthen its capabilities across the entire lifecycle of an aircraft.

James Meyler, chief executive of ORIX Aviation, said AerFin had built "a leading aviation aftermarket platform" supported by extensive technical expertise and an international customer base.

The sale also marks the exit of Danish private equity investor CataCap, which had been the majority shareholder in AerFin.

Founder Bob James said he was pleased to see the business move to what he described as "a long-term home", capable of supporting AerFin's next phase of expansion.

"I recognise a strong leadership team and a long-term home when I see one," James said.

#165. Pound rises to its highest level since the Brexit referendum

The pound has risen to its highest overall level since the Brexit referendum over a decade ago.

Sterling has risen notably in recent weeks against the euro and the dollar, ahead of an expected rise in interest rates.

The most-watched measure of the value of the British currency against a basket of 18 of the world's main currencies this week reached its joint highest level since the June 2016 referendum, according to Bank of England figures.

The value reached 86.4 on an index where 100 equals the value in 2005. The Bank will officially confirm the calculation on Thursday.

The value of sterling fell significantly after the Brexit referendum in June 2016, and reached further lows during the Brexit process, the Covid pandemic and the 2022 mini-Budget.

Some traders said the UK was being seen in a different light given relative political stability, solid growth and the rapprochement with the European Union.

Sterling has responded strongly to Brexit developments and political uncertainty in recent years.

Jim O'Neill, an economist and former Treasury minister, said: "I'm not surprised. People were, and to a lesser extent, are, underestimating the improving momentum in the UK."

A stronger pound can help rein in inflation, and increase buying power for consumers on imports and on holidays.

UK growth so far this year has outperformed all forecasts, but there are signs that higher energy and fuel prices have now hit consumer confidence.

The Bank's nine-member Monetary Policy Committee faces an important decision on interest rates on Thursday, with the first rise in three years widely expected.

The pound was up 0.13% against the dollar at $1.3495 and 0.16% lower against the euro at â¬1.1979 as of 04:15 GMT on Thursday.

Interest rates expected to rise for first time in over three years

#162. Inside the card-grading empire shaping the price of Pokémon cards

Collectors may have more grading options than ever, but many still turn to Professional Sports Authenticator, or PSA — even as the industry leader works through a backlog of millions of cards.

Demand for grading — an act of verifying a card’s authenticity and condition — has grown as collectors increasingly treat trading and vintage sports cards as alternative investments.

PSA reported a backlog of 7.2 million items on Oct. 20, down from 12.4 million in July. The company suspended cheaper submission services in June to ease the backlog before reopening its Value service on Oct. 29.

Even so, some sellers would rather wait for a PSA label than send their cards to a competitor, dealers told CNBC.

“It really all comes down to secondary market values,” said Geoff Wilson, founder and CEO of CardsHQ, which operates one of the world's largest sports card shops.

PSA-graded cards typically command higher prices than equivalent grades from competitors, Wilson said, giving collectors and investors an incentive to choose PSA in hopes of a stronger return.

The financial difference can be substantial.

A 1999 Base Set Charizard graded PSA 9 sold for about $3,440 on eBay on Sept. 29, compared with roughly $1,890 for a CGC 9 copy sold the same day, according to CNBC's review of PriceCharting records.

That pricing gap reinforces PSA's lead and gives its decisions about fees and submission services considerable influence over the resale market.

PSA had not responded to CNBC’s detailed questions at the time of publication. In published updates, the company has attributed service restrictions and pricing changes to demand exceeding capacity, and outlined investments intended to reduce the backlog.

Grading companies verify a card’s authenticity, assess its condition and seal it in a protective plastic case, known as a slab. PSA uses a 10-point scale, with its highest grade, Gem Mint 10, reserved for cards it describes as "virtually perfect."

Like a credit rating in bond markets, a card grade gives buyers and sellers a common benchmark for assessing condition.

That can reduce friction in transactions by giving both sides a reference point based on past sales of the same card at the same grade.

That capability has helped open the trading-card market to more global participation and made cross-border transactions easier, according to an unnamed spokesperson for Singapore-based submission dealer Oxley Grading.

The spokesperson attributed PSA’s position as the industry’s “gold standard” to its early start, brand recognition and extensive history of completed sales.

“They've just built up such a strong branding and a lot of sales records,” the spokesperson said.

They estimated that roughly 90% of the graded cards offered to Oxley Grading for purchase carry PSA’s label.

The dominance creates a self-reinforcing cycle: buyers seek out PSA-graded cards, giving sellers an incentive to use the company. The abundance of PSA slabs in shops and online marketplaces further reinforces the brand's visibility and demand.

For a seller choosing a grader, that established resale demand can outweigh a competitor’s lower fees or faster turnaround times.

PSA sits within a larger collectibles business.

Its parent company, Collectors, also owns rival grading brands Beckett and SGC, pricing-data platform Card Ladder, the PSA Vault and financial services. The vault links storage with selling through external marketplaces, including eBay.

Together, those businesses span much of a card’s commercial journey — grading, pricing, storage, sales and even financing against collectibles.

Collectors' three card-grading brands accounted for roughly 71% of August grading volume among the major firms tracked by industry analyst GemRate. PSA alone represented about 65%, while the next-largest player, CGC, represented about 28%.

Collectors' consolidation has also drawn legal scrutiny.

A proposed class action filed in April challenges Collectors’ acquisitions of SGC and Beckett, alleging that the deals unlawfully reduced competition in card grading. The allegations have not been proven in court.

Collectors has defended the Beckett acquisition as a way to strengthen a trusted brand while preserving collectors’ ability to choose the grading service that best suits their items.

“We believe that strong, well-supported brands help grow access to the hobby,” Collectors CEO Nat Turner said in the company's 2025 acquisition announcement.

For sellers who prioritize PSA's resale premium, changes to its fees and submission services can alter the economics of grading a card.

PSA raised fees across several existing tiers in February, including Value Bulk from $21.99 to $24.99 and Value from $27.99 to $32.99. It also lengthened estimated turnaround times for some services, saying the changes would help balance submission demand with grading capacity.

The suspension of cheaper tiers in June further narrowed affordable options, while higher costs have made customers more selective about which cards they submit, according to Oxley Grading's spokesperson.

Meanwhile, Wilson said the restrictions have particularly affected collectors and dealers who buy cards ungraded, send them to PSA and then resell them at a higher price.

For cards worth a few hundred dollars or less, that strategy has become harder to justify, he said.

“You're seeing more raw cards bought and sold at card shows,” Wilson added, using the industry term for ungraded cards.

PSA has acknowledged that customers are waiting too long.

In an Oct. 28 update, the company said submissions in April and May were 2.5 times the volume received in the same months a year earlier. It also announced shorter turnaround estimates for Standard and Priority services, while saying new Value submissions would be processed behind existing orders.

“We have added an incredible amount of capacity, but we still have work to do,” Ryan Hoge, president of PSA and grading at Collectors, said in the statement.

For Wilson, those investments in capacity are part of PSA's appeal.

“They've made the collector experience better by doing that,” he said.

The leading graders' dominance may leave collectors with little room to push back.

Collectors' preference for PSA's resale value can make switching graders less attractive, the Oxley Grading's spokesperson said, adding, "PSA's lead comes from resale demand and liquidity, and other labels are still building that."

Overseas customers can face fewer practical grading options as many major graders are based in the U.S., Amber Chuah, operations manager of trading-card platform TCG Marketplace, told CNBC.

The complaints she hears most often involve long waits and disappointment when cards receive lower grades than customers expected.

Challenging a grade can require another round of shipping and waiting, discouraging some collectors from disputing the result, she added.

"If you're really keen on selling, that's a lot of time."

PSA did not respond to CNBC's questions about the sources' claims.

PSA’s terms allow customers to request a paid review of disputed grades, with customers responsible for grading and applicable shipping fees. The company also offers defect notes for eligible submissions to provide more detail on how a grade was determined.

CardsHQ's Wilson said some variation between assessments is an inherent challenge in a process that involves human judgment.

Wilson pushes back against the suggestion that PSA’s position leaves customers without alternatives.

“First of all, there are alternatives,” he said, pointing to CGC, Beckett and smaller grading firms such as TAG.

CGC’s grading volume in August was more than double its level a year earlier, according to GemRate, showing that rival graders are attracting business. Beckett also recorded strong growth, although it is owned by Collectors.

Oxley Grading's spokesperson said there may be room for lower-cost services aimed at collectors who care more about a card’s artwork, sentimental value or preservation than maximizing its resale value.

Competing for sellers focused on the strongest financial return remains more difficult.

Building comparable resale acceptance would require substantial investment, with no guarantee that buyers would ultimately value a newcomer's label as highly as PSA's, the spokesperson said.

For Wilson, broader acceptance in the resale market is key to shifting that balance.

“If the market as a whole starts to respect the value of the other grading companies' cards more, then collectors will increasingly submit to those companies,” he said.

#161. Second buyer makes rescue bid for Poundland as retailer faces collapse

A second buyer has made a rescue bid for Poundland as the discount retailer teeters on the brink of collapse, with thousands of jobs at risk.

London-based Xyra Group has offered to pay off half of Poundland's outstanding debts - a key sticking point in takeover talks so far - immediately. The rest would be deferred.

With Poundland close to collapse, US-based owners Gordon Brothers have already rejected a rival bid from an Irish property group which would have saved its 12,000 staff.

Gordon Brothers said the business has faced years of instability and "remains challenged and vulnerable".

Sources familiar with the offer said Xyra is offering to immediately pay off Â£12.5m of an outstanding Â£30m loan on Poundland's books.

The offer came as Business Secretary Jonathan Reynolds held crunch talks with Gordon Brothers about the future of the business, urging the investment firm to protect staff however possible.

Gordon Brothers, which bought Poundland for Â£1 last June, committed at the meeting to prioritise offers which protect its shops and workers.

The company said: "We are pursuing all avenues through the sale process to find a solution which protects as many jobs as possible and preserves value. Work continues at pace, engaging in active discussions on offers with interested parties."

Xyra is offering to pay off a higher proportion of Poundland's outstanding debts than Martin Property Group, which previously submitted a bid that Gordon Brothers rejected.

But doubts remain about the credibility of Xyra's offer, as there is little evidence they meet Gordon Brothers' tests, including whether they have the money and whether they would be good long-term owners of Poundland.

Sources pointed to the appointment of Andrew Brode to Xyra's board as a sign of its credibility. Brode works for RWS - a company that provides AI solutions for businesses.

Judith MacKenzie, head of Downing Fund Managers, told the BBC Brode is "well known and very credible" among small businesses in the UK.

But she said there is "hardly any information" publicly available about Xyra. "So, although some of the people look good, I can't find any evidence to say that they've got the money to back it up yet," MacKenzie added.

Gordon Brothers said: "Our intent throughout the comprehensive sale process has been to protect as many jobs as possible and preserve value in order to deliver the best available outcome for all the people, creditors and local communities who care about this business as much as we do."

Both Reynolds and Prime Minister Andy Burnham have questioned why a deal for the future of Poundland has not yet been made.

Though Xyra made an offer to take over the whole business, it is unclear whether it would keep Poundland together as its owner or break the company up.

Several Poundland suppliers have said they would also go bust if the retailer collapses. Its founder Steve Smith said two or three thousand suppliers to the firm, those who solely work for Poundland, would be caught up in the collapse.

He added that not only would Poundland's 12,000 staff be affected in that case, but potentially up to 30,000 overall.

#160. Moderna raises 2026 outlook as CEO says newer vaccines are gaining traction

Moderna on Friday smashed third-quarter revenue and earnings estimates and hiked its outlook for full-year sales growth, as some of its newer products gain momentum and Covid vaccine revenue outperforms.

Shares of Moderna rose roughly 3% in premarket trading on Friday.

The biotech company now expects 2026 revenue growth of 15% to 25%, up from its previous guidance of up to 10%.

"The new product and the diversification of geography is what has led to such a strong Q3," Moderna CEO Stéphane Bancel said in an interview ahead of the results.

For example, he said the company is seeing the "full-year impact" of its next-generation mRNA Covid vaccine, which won U.S. approval in May 2025. Bancel noted that Covid is a seasonal business and vaccine contracts are typically established in the first quarter, so Moderna's shot only had a "small launch" to start with last year.

Moderna's new stand-alone flu shot, which was approved in the U.S. in August, will similarly have a "limited launch" in the country in 2026 given the timing, he said.

In a separate interview on CNBC's "Squawk Box," Bancel said Moderna's geographic expansion in Canada, the U.K. and Asia helped boost results. The company's work to lower costs also resulted in a profit in the quarter, he added.

"It's the sales that are stronger and the costs that are also lower," Bancel said.

Here's what Moderna reported for the third quarter compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Moderna's third-quarter revenue jumped 29% to $1.31 billion, boosted by higher Covid vaccine sales in international markets and helped by deliveries under long-term government partnerships. Revenue was $780 million in the U.S. and $533 million in international markets.

The company posted a net income of $162 million, or 38 cents per share, for the third quarter. That compares with a net loss of $200 million, or a loss of 51 cents per share, reported for the year-ago period.

The results come weeks after Moderna and Merck unveiled promising late-stage data on their experimental personalized cancer vaccine in combination with immunotherapy Keytruda in certain melanoma patients. Despite the positive results, investors battered Moderna's stock after part of the data was leaked on social media just days before trial investigators were set to present it at a European medical conference.

Bancel called it a "surprising reaction," but said that the companies also saw a positive response from others on Wall Street, including long-term investors and analysts who examined the full data in depth. He added that the doctors in the field "were so impressed with the data," and many expressed that they hope to use it in their day-to-day practice.

Moderna's stock has skyrocketed more than 700% this year, largely on optimism about the potential for the shot.

#159. Drunk and dangerous e-bike riders face on-the-spot fines under crackdown

People riding drunk or dangerously on e-bikes â and those who jump red lights - could face on-the-spot fines under plans to curb anti-social behaviour.

Firms which run shared e-bike schemes will also be expected to manage their fleets better and provide designated parking areas to keep pavements safe.

They could also be required to take out insurance so people can claim compensation if they are hit by someone on a shared bike.

It's part of a crackdown on what ministers called the "wild west" of e-biking â with a patchwork of rules across the country often leaving the streets unsafe for pedestrians.

A consultation launched on Thursday sets out proposed national standards to make journeys safer for everyone and keep pavements clear, particularly for wheelchair users and families with prams.

The first e-bikes were launched in Exeter in 2016 and arrived in London a year later, growing to become a popular commuter option.

According to the Greater London Authority, 37 million trips were taken on dockless e-bikes last year â a third more than the previous year.

Shared e-bikes - such as Lime, Voi, Beryl, Dott, Forest and Bolt - make short journeys quick and convenient.

While some areas enforce rules that mean shared bikes can only be left in specific areas, others have looser rules.

Under the government's proposals, local leaders will be able to license schemes in their area, allowing them to enforce minimum standards and better integrate e-bikes into existing transport networks.

Measures could include requiring operators to have a parking plan and to manage fleet sizes and operating areas, so bikes aren't overcrowding streets.

The consultation will also consider ways to tackle antisocial and illegal behaviour, to prevent users from riding shared cycles drunk, underage, or on pavements. There will also be clear consequences set out â such as fines - for this type of behaviour.

The government is also consulting on whether local leaders should have extra powers, including setting fees and requirements for operators to share data to improve enforcement and journey planning.

The consultation will last for eight weeks after which the government will draw up legislation to implement the changes.

'Dumped e-scooters force blind people into road'

Red light jumping cyclists offered film over fine

'Our lives will never be the same again after Lime bike crash'

Prime Minister Andy Burnham said the e-bike clampdown was a "small but important everyday fix" which would make streets safer.

Transport Secretary Heidi Alexander said the government wanted e-bike schemes to "grow in popularity" but acknowledged they were "causing a little bit of nuisance at the moment".

Asked how punishments for dangerous cycling could be enforced, Alexander said licensing rules could allow for fines but added: "What you can't do is legislate for every eventuality, you can't legislate for idiots."

"The police enforce the rules of the road to stop cyclists when they are behaving recklessly but police have got a lot on their plate so individuals have to take some responsibility," she added.

Bosses at Lime, Voi and Bolt all welcomed the proposals, adding that consistent standards being applied across the country would provide clarity and certainty for operators, riders and the general public.

Mike Wordingham from the Royal National Institute for the Blind said 92% of blind and partially sighted people had been forced to walk into roads due to e-bikes blocking pavements.

He welcomed the government's announced but warned that tackling rider behaviour would be "difficult" and said a locally-led approach shouldn't lead to "a patchwork of regulations and inconsistency in implementing them".

#158. Democrats amp up Trump investigation threats, Blanche counters he may probe lawmakers

Top Democrats are ramping up oversight of the Trump administration and businesses it has partnered with, while Attorney General Todd Blanche warned he could counterinvestigate lawmakers conducting probes.

Democrats romped to majorities in both the House and the Senate during Tuesday's midterm elections, putting them in position to seize control of committee gavels and subpoena power in January. The electoral rout leaves Democrats with at least 54 Senate seats and 254 seats in the House of Representatives as votes continue to be counted in several states.

Democrats vowed to investigate both President Donald Trump's White House and businesses that have worked with the administration. CNBC reported on Thursday that Democrats are expected to take an aggressive approach to probing businesses, especially those companies with ties to the Trump family. The technology, cryptocurrency and prediction market industries, among others, could immediately be in the crosshairs.

Elizabeth Warren, the incoming chair of the Senate Banking Committee, on CBS' "Face the Nation" on Sunday. "The American people are just damn sick of this."

Blanche, Trump's former personal attorney, whom the Senate narrowly confirmed as the nation's top cop earlier this year, took umbrage at the Democrats' plans for investigations and said he may launch his own investigations against members of Congress.

Elissa Slotkin, D-Mich., who said during a University of Chicago event that she wants "young people coming out of school to say, 'Should I go work for the Trump administration?'...I want them to be like, 'Lord, that might be buying myself a ton of problems that I don't want. And so let me avoid participating in that.' I want that deterrence message sent."

Trump has ordered the Justice Department to investigate Democrats over the comments.

Blanche also took aim at Rep. Robert Garcia, D-Calif., the likely incoming chair of the House Oversight Committee — appearing to caution lawmakers who sit on the panel. Garcia warned the administration and business leaders to "lawyer up" after the election.

"I would be very worried if I was a member on that committee," Blanche said.

Garcia shot back, saying on CNN: "What Todd Blanche is doing right now is a threat."

"He's trying to stop us from doing real oversight," Garcia said. "He is the person that should be reading the Constitution, because the Constitution makes it very clear that it's our job in Congress to conduct oversight of the executive and the administration."

#157. Three ways the interest rate rise could affect you

Interest rates have been raised for the first time in more than three years - and it could be good and bad news for you.

Here are three ways the increase to 4% from 3.75% could affect you.

Rate-setters at the Bank make changes to interest rates to fulfil their role of keeping price rises at a steady level.

Higher rates are meant to encourage people to spend less, reducing demand for goods and services and limiting price rises.

This impacts businesses in two ways - less money to spend among their customers may mean they sell less, but also the cost of borrowing to invest as a company goes up.

Many businesses, particularly small ones, have variable rate loans which are influenced by the Bank rate.

Firms say higher rates limit their ability to offer new jobs and wage rises.

Mitchell Barnes, founder and chief executive of Ryse 3D, which manufactures 3D parts for the defence and automotive sectors, said the "reality" of rising interest rates was to look at the cost of employees and people.

That could include greater use of robots rather than workers.

"It's going to cause issues with, I suppose, employment across all sectors for people," he said.

"You're going to see reduced employment, more automation, more robotics being put into facilities, just in order to increase efficiency and make that money go further."

The Bank rate - and where it is expected to go in future - has a significant impact on the interest rates customers are offered on savings products.

So, if you're saving up for something quite expensive, like a holiday, or very expensive, like a home, then a rate rise could be good news.

When interest rates were low throughout the 2010s, the returns for savers were poor, but borrowing costs were low too.

The answer then and now, according to experts, is to make sure you move your savings to benefit from the best possible rates.

What's happening to UK interest rates and what does it mean for mortgages?

You need Â£17,000 for a first home - here's how to save it

Faisal Islam: How high will interest rates go?

Leaving your savings in an old account (a product not offered to new customers), rather than shopping around, can mean you are penalised for your loyalty.

"Savers have endured years of unrewarded loyalty, with those leaving [a Â£20,000 cash pot] in closed accounts losing out on an extra Â£139 over the past three years compared to on-sale easy access accounts," said Rachel Springall, from financial information service Moneyfacts.

She also said locking away your money for longer, with a fixed-term deal, is usually more lucrative.

The other flipside is that renting while you save could get more expensive, because a rising Bank rate may well increase costs for landlords.

The big losers from a rate rise are those who borrow money, and specifically those on variable rate mortgages.

About 500,000 homeowners have a mortgage that "tracks" the Bank of England's rate.

Any rise in the Bank rate means an increase in the monthly repayments on their outstanding loan. For a typical loan, the cost is going up by about Â£31 a month now the Bank has raised the base rate, but it depends on the size of people's loans.

An additional 500,000 homeowners on standard variable rates (SVRs) must wait to see if their lender chooses to pass on any Bank rate rise. Typically, this would mean a Â£14 increase per month in repayments for a 0.25 percentage points rates rise.

If you, like 87% of mortgage-holders, have a fixed-rate deal, then the payments will stay the same until the end of your current deal, which is usually two or five years.

While monthly payments are not immediately affected by a rate change, future deals are. Lenders had already "priced in" the latest rate rise when quoting new deals. Expectations for the Bank rate into next year are crucial on what they offer next.

The average rate on fixed deals are much higher now than was predicted at the start of the year. It means those remortgaging, possibly after the post-Covid boom in the housing market, are facing the prospect of higher mortgage payments.

Some people have been choosing to move onto tracker deals instead, taking a view - including some risk - of the Bank rate not moving towards a series of further hikes.

Do you have a mortgage, a credit card, a car finance agreement? How are interest rates affecting you?

#156. How Democrats will try to end Trump's tariffs

The battle for Congress is over. The war over President Donald Trump's tariffs is about to heat up.

The knotted web of tariffs that Trump spent the last two years weaving could soon face its biggest threat, as Democrats prepare to unload their new legislative firepower against the Republican president's signature policy.

Democrats have made no secret of their plans to bombard Trump and his associates with investigations as soon as the next Congress is sworn in. They are also preparing a first-100-hours legislative blitz, putting forward a series of bills that would unwind much of Trump's agenda.

Both prongs of attack will take aim at Trump's unprecedented use of tariffs, which Democrats have quickly identified as a main target following their landslide victory in Tuesday's midterm elections.

Ending Trump's tariff regime, and especially his trade war against Canada, is something Democrats "absolutely have to do immediately," Sen.-elect Abdul El-Sayed, D-Mich., told MS NOW's "Morning Joe" Thursday.

Rep. Hakeem Jeffries of New York, the Democratic minority leader who could be elected House speaker in the next Congress, has made clear that ending Trump's tariffs is a top priority.

"We should restore congressional authority as it relates to tariffs, because the Trump tariffs are making life more expensive for the American people," he said at a press conference in late August.

If picked as speaker, Jeffries would speed legislation to the House floor that reasserts congressional authority over trade.

Democrats are also laying the groundwork for investigations into Trump's tariffs, which the party folded into its winning campaign message linking high costs with alleged Trumpworld corruption.

Rep. Jamie Raskin of Maryland, the top Democrat on the House Judiciary Committee, on Oct. 28 asked Trump to hand over records related to tariff exemptions. Trump, Raskin alleges, has wielded the exemption power to cut "sweetheart deals" for industries whose leaders have plied the president with gifts.

The plans are likely to ratchet up the pressure, both legally and politically, that Trump's tariffs were already facing.

The administration is already embroiled in multiple lawsuits challenging different parts of the government's tariff policy.

The most recent suit, filed Oct. 20, targets Trump's use of a Great Depression-era law to impose 50% tariffs on a range of Canadian goods.

Another court case accuses Trump of trying to recreate the near-global "liberation day" tariffs that the Supreme Court struck down as illegal in late February. The administration has since been ordered to refund $166 billion in tariff revenue it had collected under the scrapped policy.

Congress, not the president, holds the power to levy taxes under the U.S. Constitution. Over decades, the legislative branch has ceded some of those powers to the executive. But Trump has asserted virtually unchecked tariff powers – which Democrats say they are intent on clawing back.

"Every day Trump insists on his indiscriminate taxes on so many of the products Americans need, he digs a deeper hole for Republicans enabling his tariff spree," said Sen. Ron Wyden of Oregon, who is poised to lead the Senate Finance Committee when Democrats take it over.

"If Democrats somehow had a veto-proof majority, or if voters shame Republicans into doing the right thing, there would be no shortage of trade policies to address," Wyden said in a statement to CNBC before Election Day.

"One of the most obvious priorities would be repealing Trump's tariffs on Canada, which are raising prices and harming communities on both sides of the border," he said. "I'll also continue to work to ensure Congress has the final say on tariffs, so future presidents can't reenact Trump's economically disastrous trade taxes."

The Wall Street Journal, citing Wyden, reported Thursday that Democrats are eyeing the Canada trade war as the "first target" in their efforts against his tariff agenda.

As Wyden's statement suggested, the Senate's rules will hamper Democrats' anti-tariff efforts.

Despite their blowout victory Tuesday night, the party will still lack a 60-vote filibuster-proof majority in the Senate, giving Republicans a crucial tool to block legislation even in their diminished state.

Trump is also poised to lean heavily on his veto pen in the next Congress, presenting a major obstacle for Democrats' legislative priorities.

Overriding a presidential veto requires two-thirds majorities in both the House and Senate, meaning that even with their historic electoral gains, Democrats would need significant Republican help to force a bill into law over Trump's objection.

Democrats may consider it worthwhile to pass legislation even if Trump vetoes it, as doing so could help them build political narratives in the 2028 election cycle. But they may have other options available.

"Absent the brute force of a veto-proof coalition, Congress might consider other political solutions, such as adding riders on 'must-pass' bills or negotiation with the administration," Kit Johnson, a senior tax manager for BDO's customs and international trade group, told CNBC in an email.

Trump has said he plans to use the veto power to force Democrats to the negotiating table. But when it comes to tariffs, it's hard to imagine what deal Trump might accept.

Trump has tried to reshape U.S. trade, foreign and economic policy around his asserted power to slap steep duties on imported goods. He describes tariffs as a win-win, praising them as both a powerful negotiating tool and a major revenue generator.

After Tuesday's election, Trump seemed to suggest tariffs were non-negotiable.

"If I took tariffs off — we have 3,000 plants under construction right now. Every single plant would stop building," Trump told CNBC's Eamon Javers in a phone call Wednesday.

Since taking office in January 2025, Trump has hiked tariffs on nearly every country on the planet, leading to multiple protracted trade wars, most notably against Canada and China. He insists Americans do not share the burden of the duties, even though tariffs by definition are taxes on foreign goods paid by those importing them. The nonprofit Tax Foundation estimates Trump's tariffs have raised the average U.S. household's tax burden by $820 in 2026.

Trump has also consistently overstated the amount of revenue the government has amassed through his tariffs. He claimed before Election Day that tariffs enabled him to offer every U.S. adult $5,000 "dividend" checks if Republicans were to win both chambers of Congress – a plan that would cost roughly $1.2 trillion. But the duties – which are collected from U.S. importers – have only brought in around $300 billion in total between January 2025 and July 2026, according to the Penn Wharton Budget Model.

What's more, about $138 billion of that total has been refunded since the Supreme Court ruling in February. Nearly $20 billion in tariff collections are still set to be refunded.

Most voters do not share Trump's rosy view of tariffs. Sixty-five percent of respondents to the CNBC Voter Poll on Nov. 3 said the duties have hurt the U.S. economy, while just 22% say they helped.

What remains to be seen is how hard Republicans in Congress will push back on Democrats' efforts.

A number of GOP lawmakers came out against the duties during the campaign, as their opponents gained ground blaming inflation on Trump's tariffs and the Iran war.

As they try to regroup and look forward to the 2028 election cycle, some Republicans – who largely supported free trade prior to Trump's political rise – may be less willing to continue aligning themselves with Trump's aggressive protectionism.

#155. Faisal Islam: How high will interest rates go?

Interest rates have risen to 4% and the Bank of England has indicated there are more rises to come. The question is how many? There is a split on the rate-setting committee here.

Three of the nine voting members referred to "rises" plural. One member voted for a bigger increase, to 4.25%. Three referred to just one more including governor Andrew Bailey.

My read on this is that the expectation is for one more rise in February. Two and done, but it depends.

It is painful for those on variable mortgages, but should not be seen as the start of a cycle of hikes.

Predicting where interest rates will go is like throwing darts at a board that constantly moves around the wall, with expectations of how long the Iran War will last and its impact on the economy and prices lurching from day to day.

The Bank's forecasts indicate inflation is heading to just under 5% next spring as a typical household energy bill surges by 27% in January to Â£2,150 a year.

Why have they raised now? High and rising inflation risks a further cycle of self fulfilling expectations of rising prices. It hasn't been seen so far, however. The war has lasted for 9 months, and led to oil prices used in the forecast averaging $108 a barrel.

And yet on the day they have announced the rate rise, the oil price is actually 13% lower than that having fallen in the run up and after the US mid term elections, reflecting expectations or guesses that President Trump will now retreat or deescalate from the Iran war.

The other notable factor is that the UK economy is performing much better than forecasts and expectations. This is underlined by the Bank of England's latest forecasts.

Growth has been 0.6%, and 0.5% in the first two quarters of the year. It is heading for 0.7%, the Bank said, between July and September, before slowing in the three months to the end of the year. Unemployment has stabilised, actually falling a little.

Some of this has fed through into a better performing currency, with sterling at its highest level since the Brexit referendum, when measured against a basket of currencies. This can help rein in inflation by making imports cheaper.

So, this is an unusual type of uncertainty. Even the expected further rise, probably in February next year is conditional on the war continuing.

The Bank of England stopped waiting for President Trump's war to end today, but perhaps the Democrats' resounding victory in the US midterm elections will stop the inflationary wave? The Bank has thrown a tentative first dart, and there is probably another to come.

#154. Tesla to wind down Cybertruck production in Texas next year to ramp Cybercab, Musk says

Tesla CEO Elon Musk on Friday said the electric vehicle maker will "be winding down" production of the Cybertruck at its Texas gigafactory around the middle of next year.

Musk, who hailed the Cybertruck as the future of the pickup truck segment, said in a post on X early Friday morning that the end of production at the massive facility would make room for manufacturing of its Cybercab robotaxi.

The Cybertruck is exclusively produced at the company's plant in Texas alongside the Tesla Model Y and two-seat Cybercab, which Musk has touted as a crucial product for the automaker moving forward.

Musk did not say whether the company would produce the Cybertruck at a different facility, but he later responded to a user on X who called the update an "end of an era" and said the Cybertruck was the "best vehicle Tesla has ever made."

"True, but the Cybertruck fleet will be around for a long time," Musk said.

Tesla began Cybertruck deliveries in late 2023. The company did not immediately respond to a request for comment from CNBC.

The Cybertruck and its polarizing design gained a niche following, but it did not achieve mass-market sales, despite Musk's pre-launch prediction that annual sales would eventually total 250,000.

"Trucks have been the same for a very long time," Musk said when the truck was unveiled in 2019. "Like 100 years. Trucks have been basically the same. We want to try something different."

While Tesla does not break out its sales by model or region, Cox Automotive estimated sales of the Cybertruck last year at 20,237 units in the U.S., down 48% from 38,965 in 2024.

One big buyer of the Cybertruck last year was Musk's aerospace and tech conglomerate, SpaceX, which spent $131 million buying up the angular steel trucks at Tesla's suggested retail price, according to a SpaceX financial filing.

The distinct look of the vehicle made some owners a target of rude gestures and more serious road rage, one owner told CNBC last May, after Musk became the biggest funder of Donald Trump's presidential campaign and led the so-called Department of Government Efficiency, an effort to slash the federal workforce and regulations.

Wired wrote in an October 2025 feature story, "There is likely no object that feels more emblematic of US president Donald Trump's return to the White House than the Tesla Cybertruck." The magazine interviewed owners who described being spit on and sworn at due to their choice of vehicle.

The Cybertruck's specifications also fell short of those Tesla advertised early on, and in some cases fell short of those offered by leading fuel-burning pickups. There have also been many quality and production problems associated with the vehicles.

The 2024 Tesla Cybertruck was the subject of 11 voluntary recalls, according to the National Highway Traffic Safety Administration website. Issues ranged from a "trapped" acceleration pedal risk that could cause unintended acceleration, to software defects and an inverter fault that could result in sudden loss of drive power.

#153. Interest rates hiked to 4% in first increase in over three years

Interest rates have risen for the first time in more than three years and the Bank of England has signalled a further increase is likely unless the US-Iran war is resolved.

Rates rose to 4% from 3.75% in a bid to curb inflation mainly driven by the conflict's impact on global oil prices.

Andrew Bailey, the Bank's governor, said the decision "shouldn't be seen as the first in an inevitable long sequence of hikes", but warned "unless the conflict in the Middle East is resolved, neither does it seem likely to be the last increase".

The higher level will increase the cost of borrowing for things such as mortgages, loans and credit cards, but will also lead to higher returns on savings.

The Bank's rate-setting committee all backed increasing interest rates, with eight out of nine voting in favour of the rise to 4%. One member wanted an even bigger increase to 4.25%.

Bailey said the hike was warranted due to higher energy prices and pointed to what happens in the Middle East as the main factor in future decisions.

"Put simply, it requires real progress towards settling the conflict in the Middle East," the governor said.

Bailey said he could "see the argument" for a further rate hike, before taking a "more conditional approach" dependent on the path of energy prices and inflation in the coming months.

However, the views of members on the rate-setting committee differed on the path forward.

Clare Lombardelli agreed there was "likely to be a case for a further increase in rates in the near term", while Megan Greene went further, suggesting "additional hikes" might be likely depending on how inflation impacted wages, prices and the jobs market.

However, Alan Taylor said the rate rise was a "a risk-management 'one-for-now' insurance hike".

Inflation, which measures the pace of price rises over time, is well below its peak of a few years ago but remains above the Bank of England's 2% target and is expected to increase further next year.

Prices rose 3.5% in the year to September 2026, largely due to higher fuel prices. Millions of households are also set to be hit by energy bill increases in January.

Higher fuel prices can lead to prices rising for a host of goods and staples as businesses pass on higher costs to customers.

But the Bank said there was "limited evidence" this had happened so far for goods, such as food, with some firms absorbing cost increases and accepting lower margins.

In an interview with the BBC, Bailey defended the decision to raise rates despite the Bank's assessment that inflation was yet to spread to other parts of the economy.

He said further increases in energy prices are putting upward pressure on inflation and "that's what we have to seek to lean against".

Patrick Bryson, from Brighton, is set to see his five-year mortgage term with a 3.51% interest rate end soon.

The best deal the 51-year-old can find is a 5.17% loan with HSBC, which means his repayments will jump from Â£1,420 to Â£1,680 a month from February.

"The only thing that bugs me about the mortgage is that after five years of paying it off we are now paying more than we were before," said the father-of-three. "That's a bit of a slap in the face."

The guitar teacher, who has a combined income of around Â£68,000 a year with his wife, who manages a care home, said the couple is feeling pressure on their finances and so have changed their shopping habits and are looking to reduce their subcription outgoings.

"Over the last year I have stopped buying wine, we don't go out to eat much anymore, we have knocked takeaways on the head. The prices are ridiculous," he said.

Three ways the interest rate rise could affect you

Faisal Islam: How high will interest rates go?

The Bank now expects inflation to peak at 4.9% in the middle of next year, higher than it originally estimated, and not return to 2% until 2028.

But in an "adverse scenario", where the US-Iran war could escalate further and send oil prices higher, inflation could reach 6.2% in 2027, it suggested.

While the UK cannot control global energy prices, the Bank raised rates, for the first time since August 2023, in order to limit the knock-on effects of high energy prices resulting in inflation going up across the board.

Its base rate is what it charges other banks and building societies to borrow money, which influences what customers for mortgages, loans, credit cards get charged, as well as savings rates.

The rate rise will see 510,000 tracker mortgage-holders see a Â£31 monthly jump in repayments, according to banking trade body UK Finance.

Homeowners on fixed deals will see no change, though future products may be affected.

However, people on variable deals will be impacted depending on how their lender reacts to the rate rise. Typically a 0.25pp rise means a Â£14 increase per month in repayments.

#151. 'I was asked if I was a crier': Your worst job interviews revealed

Watch: What was your worst ever job interview?

Tiara Allen arrived on time to interview for a retail job. Her interviewer didn't.

She waited for around 40 minutes in a shopping centre while the interviewer's co-worker called them "two or three times".

When they finally showed up, Tiara says she was told it was because the meeting wasn't in the schedule.

She needed work at the time, so took the job when she was offered it, but says she would handle the situation differently now.

"I would not take a job if they took that long," she says, adding that she believed her treatment during the interview was a sign of how the firm tended to treat staff.

Tiara Allen says she took the job despite being made to wait 40 minutes before the interview because she needed work

Tiara is far from alone. She is one of many people who spoke to the BBC about their worst job interview experiences after we wrote about the one interview question that leaves you feeling uncomfortable.

While those situations can be hard to deal with in the moment, they can also teach us a lot about the world of work.

During an interview for an assistant at a book publisher in New York, Chas Edwards was asked a question he wasn't expecting.

"I was asked for my first job if I was a crier," he recalls.

Thinking the interviewer was being funny, Chas replied that he didn't cry. He was later told that this was why he got the job.

"Her last assistant had cried â too often, I guess? â and she wanted someone who didn't cry. So she was being serious and I thought it was a joke," he says.

It was a bad sign. Chas says it was "a terrible, terrible job with a miserable boss" and while he managed to make it through 13 months there without crying he was "crying inside".

"I set the world record for working for this person. No-one had ever lasted three months with her before," he recalls.

Despite all this, Chas says he is in some ways grateful the experience was so awful.

"Because every job since then, even the bad ones, are not that bad," he says, adding that he can "cry anywhere now".

Chas Edwards says he thought the interviewer's question for his first job was a joke

Corinne Card also found herself floored when the first question she was asked in an interview for an administrative job was if she liked working with people.

When she replied yes, she was told that the job wouldn't be for her and the interview ended there.

"I don't know if it was a deliberate trap. Because who would answer 'I don't like working with people'?"

Corinne Mills, joint managing director of Personal Career Management, says that it can be worth asking an interviewer to clarify any weird questions, perhaps by repeating the question back to them.

However, if a line of questioning is really off-putting, she says it can also be a sign of what it's like to work there, saying that interviews are also about candidates judging the company.

Meanwhile, other questions are not just annoying â they are illegal.

Discrimination against a protected characteristic is against the law, external, meaning an employer cannot treat you less favourably based on your age, disability, gender reassignment, marriage, pregnancy, race, religion, sex, or sexual orientation.

Employment lawyer Roy Magara says that women being asked if they intend on having children in an interview is a "quite common" kind of bias. He says he has also seen a case where an older candidate was told he didn't get the job after an interview because he didn't fit the "vibe" of a younger team.

If you think these sorts of biases took place in your interview, Roy says you can send the company a subject access request, external to see how they discussed your application internally.

Worst-case scenarios like these can make interviews feel very difficult, especially considering the control employers have over candidates.

Euan Cameron, co-founder of hiring software Willo, says that even with this power imbalance it's important not to overthink what an interview is.

"An interview is just a conversation between two people," he says.

Of course, as in Tiara's case, it helps when both of those people show up.

#149. Cathie Wood 'always feels better when the market is worried' — here's why

Ark Invest CEO Cathie Wood is shrugging off this week's market jitters, telling CNBC on Friday that concerns among investors actually reinforce her optimism that further gains lie ahead.

U.S. equities sold off sharply on Thursday, reversing the initial rally seen in the wake of the midterm elections. AI-related stocks led the rout, sending the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite into correction territory. On Friday, tech stocks staged a tentative recovery.

Much of the sell-off on Thursday was driven by concerns that a Democrat-led Congress would lead to a slowdown in data center construction. But concerns about artificial intelligence – particularly when it comes to capital expenditure, cash flow, debt and circular financing – have sparked multiple bouts of volatility over the course of this year.

Wood, whose firm manages various funds focused on tech and innovation, told CNBC there was a "wall of worry" pervading financial markets right now. But her decades of experience as an investor give her a somewhat unconventional view of the state of play.

"I experienced the tech and telecom bubble, and you know there really wasn't much worry about it," she said. "It was the 80s and the 90s, and it was a bull market, and people were just assuming this would go to the moon, that is not how this [the AI trade] has evolved."

She explained that "from the beginning," many investors have been concerned about how much firms are spending on AI development, how rapidly the spending is taking place, and how the hyperscalers have moved from extreme levels of positive free cash flow to negative free cash flow.

"So it's been unsettling the markets for quite some time," she said. "I always feel better when the market is worried, because that tends to suggest a more durable bull market, and I don't think we've derailed this bull market."

In 2025, her flagship Ark Innovation ETF gained more than 35%, far outperforming the S&P 500's 16.4% rise and the Nasdaq Composite's 20.5% rally.

Asked on Friday whether markets were anywhere near the end of the current bull run, Wood replied: "I don't think so."

"I think we're in the middle of a technology revolution," she said, pointing to the development of technologies like robotaxis and AI agents.

One thing that did risk becoming a "showstopper," Wood noted, was the rising cost of capital.

Interest rates and bond yields have risen sharply this year as the U.S.-Iran war pushes inflation higher across the globe. Yields on corporate bonds have been rising too, with creditors demanding greater compensation for lending to companies.

"This correction is partly caused by what's going on in the bond markets," Wood said on Friday.

"I had been saying for quite some time that the wall of worry has been built by senior leaders in the financial world who were young during the bubble," she said.

"It's the elder statesmen and women of the industry who are keeping this wall of worry in place, and that's not bad. Now you can add the bond investor to that. Bond investors in the old days weren't necessary for technology. It was software. It was low fixed cost. It was viral apps, very high margins. [AI] is high fixed cost, so bond investors are experiencing or taking a journey into the technology world, really that they haven't very much before."

But she noted that AI hyperscalers were somewhat insulated by opportunities for growth.

"If a company thinks that taking on debt is going to accelerate its momentum, meaning its growth rate, it hardly matters what happens to interest rates for those companies," Wood explained.

Wood said her advice to investors in the current volatile environment was to identify their high-conviction positions and double down on them as the market shifts.

"When you see a rally in a volatile environment, take some profits. When you see a sell-off, you'll have the psychological wherewithal if you have taken profits to move back in," she said.

"That's what we do. So what we do during periods like this is concentrate towards our highest conviction names. So that's what we've been doing in the last week or so. Our SpaceX position has gone up. If anything, the controversy around data centers on Earth is giving data centers in space a bigger opportunity."

In 2027, Wood said she expects the equity rally to continue.

"This is two steps forward, one step back," she said of the post-election turbulence. "The revolution will become clearer and clearer next year, and I think the market will do well."

#148. AI stocks climb after trillions wiped from market values

Shares in artificial intelligence and technology firms have recovered slightly from an enormous sell-off that wiped trillions of dollars off stock values.

The tech-heavy Nasdaq stock index closed in positive territory following a market rout that began in the US on Thursday before spilling over to Asian and European markets into Friday.

Investors had been selling off tech shares in part due to concerns the Democrat victory in the midterm elections would mean slower artificial intelligence development.

However the latest US employment figures added to a mixed picture for American markets on Friday morning, with an unexpectedly strong 165,000 jobs added to the economy in September.

On Thursday, businesses closely linked to artificial intelligence development took a hammering on stock markets, particularly chip and semiconductor makers.

But the Nasdaq Composite bounced back slightly on Friday, ending the day up 1.1%, partly reversing some of its 5.5% fall.

The S&P 500, which tracks the share prices of the largest publicly-traded US companies, closed up 0.3%, while the Dow Jones Industrial Average was flat through the day before ending slightly down 0.4%.

Companies that lost hundreds of billions in market value recovered some ground on Friday, including Nvidia, SpaceX and Tesla.

However, stock markets in Asia and Europe fared worse.

The Kospi in South Korea closed down 6% on Friday, with shares in chipmaker SK Hynix plunging more than 10%. European markets also fell, with the main indexes in Germany, France and Spain ending the day down.

In the UK, the FTSE 100, which tracks the largest companies on the London Stock Exchange, declined 1.01%.

In addition to the recent market volatility, UK traders were also digesting the latest Bank of England decision to raise interest rates and the signal that another rate rise could be on the cards to curb rising prices.

The market fluctuations also come after the sweeping Democrat victory in the midterm elections.

A number of Democrats campaigned for new rules and regulations for artificial intelligence (AI) and data centres, and the party has since won control of both houses of congress as well as key contests in states that have been part of the AI data centre race.

US President Donald Trump on Friday reiterated his supported for what he calls "super intelligence", saying only "foolish leadership" would "say no to the riches they bring".

However, there have also been concerns over whether the enormous amounts of money being spent on AI will pay off.

Russ Shaw, founder of global tech advocacy firm Tech London Advocates, said the election result was sending a message that a rethink was needed on AI.

"The incoming Congress is going to really focus on AI in a very dramatic way," he told the BBC's Business Today.

"I'm a big fan of AI, but I'm watching what these companies are doing, and they're not communicating effectively at all, particularly in the US, about what the benefits of AI are all about."

Pierre BaquÃ©, chief executive of AI platform Neural Concept, said the share movements were a "necessary market correction" that reflected a legitimate concern about the amount of money being poured into AI infrastructure.

"The industry must move beyond unchecked infrastructure investment and demonstrate greater financial discipline," he said.

"We are entering a critical transition phase, a market purgatory where raw tech must prove its widespread economic value."

Dan Coatsworth, head of markets at AJ Bell, said it had been a brutal week for markets, and the key question was whether the jump in the S&P 500 and Nasdaq would be sustained.

"Once investors have had the weekend to catch their breath, next week could give a clearer indication of whether the tech trade is going down the pan or fighting back."

Could the US midterms put a pin in the AI bubble?

Elon Musk appears to signal the end of the road for Cybertruck

As well as the fallout of the midterms and the plunge in artificial intelligence stocks, US traders will also be digesting the latest jobs figures.

And those numbers were better than expected: US employers added 165,000 new jobs in October, well above the average monthly increase and more than double what analysts had expected.

Overall the unemployment rate, which counts the number of people who are out of work but actively looking for a job, eased slightly to 4.1% from 4.2% in September.

The Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS) said the unemployment rate has remained in the narrow range of 4.1% to 4.3% since March.

Neil Birrell, chief investment officer at investment firm Premier Miton, said while the jobs figures may feel like second billing to the market movements they were still important.

"The US economy is running hard which may mean the risks the markets are worrying about at the moment are not such a risk to the real economy as is being feared," he said.

#146. PM tells firms to publish salaries in job adverts ahead of new law

Andy Burnham says it isn't fair if an employer 'holds all the cards'

Businesses should publish salaries in job adverts now, Prime Minister Andy Burnham is telling firms, rather than wait for a new law to come in that will force them to do so.

The government is planning to make pay details compulsory in adverts, with legislation on the issue being brought forward "as soon as possible".

It argues the change will help jobseekers, particularly those applying for their first job, and could help prevent pay discrimination. However, one industry group said setting pay was a complex process and regulating adverts was not always suitable.

It comes a week after the UK's equality watchdog said employees should legally be able to find out what their colleagues are earning.

Burnham said the current system felt "weighted against the applicant" and the changes were aimed at "tilting the scales back".

"It can be quite soul destroying for young people these days, applying for jobs when the salary is not listed but sometimes they're not getting a reply," he told the BBC.

The prime minister added that publishing salaries in adverts was also important for closing the gender pay gap.

He was also challenged over whether MPs should be disclosing salaries when advertising for staff in their offices.

A number of politicians are currently advertising for roles without giving an expected salary., external

Burnham said: "It would apply completely to them too and the call I'm making today is, before the law changes do it voluntarily because it's the right thing to do."

The Cabinet Office launched a consultation into pay and reducing discrimination in the summer, and the government said there had been "overwhelming support" for greater transparency, with 94% of respondents backing the move.

"Whether you're a young person starting out or a parent trying to support your family, you should know what a job pays before spending your time and effort applying for it," Burnham said.

The government said the move was part of attempts to reduce the nearly one million young people not in education, employment or training - or "Neet" - ahead of the final report of the Milburn Review into youth inactivity, which is due in the coming weeks.

Publishing salary details upfront could "expose unjustified differences in pay, give workers greater confidence to challenge discrimination and prevent disagreements over pay escalating into disputes".

The proposals are similar to measures being introduced in the European Union., external

Employers in Europe will have to disclose the initial pay or range for new roles, which must either be included in a published job advert or shared before an interview.

According to data from UK jobs website Adzuna, external, 51% of the adverts on its site in September had salary information, but the proportion has been falling in recent years.

Adzuna co-founder Andrew Hunter said being open about pay "should be the norm, not the exception".

He added that employers who put a salary on a job advert "attract more applicants, and better-suited ones".

Previous research from the CIPD, external, an industry body for human resources managers, has indicated that the public sector and charities are more likely to reveal pay information that private firms, while pay ranges were more common than specific salaries.

But it found not all employers were "fully transparent", with pay ranges sometimes wider than Â£10,000.

CIPD chief executive Neil Carberry said that while, in principle, giving salary details was "a good thing", in practice it was "more complicated".

"There isn't always a single fixed figure, and employers will want to set pay based on a candidate's skills, experience and potential."

Carberry added the process was complex, saying that drafting job adverts is "best left to good employer practice rather than government regulation".

The government has said it will set out further details on what information will be required and how the legislation will be enforced "in due course".

Conservative shadow business secretary Julia Lopez said: "At a time when employers are finding it harder and harder to take on staff, Labour have decided to make their lives even more difficult.

"It should be up to businesses to decide what information they advertise about job openings, and to decide how to attract the best applicants.

"As long as they are not being dishonest, misleading or discriminatory, it should not be a matter for the government."

Last week, the Equality and Human Rights Commission (EHRC), said publishing salaries in job adverts would be a "positive step".

It also said bosses should be required to respond to questionnaires about colleagues' pay to cut pay discrimination.

At present, employers do not have to respond to employee requests for salary information about their co-workers.

The EHRC also said employers should be prevented from asking about current salary when offering a job to stop a history of discrimination from continuing.

Employers in the UK are currently allowed to ask you how much you get paid in your current role, but applicants are under no obligation to tell them.
