#145. Small retailers brace for 'huge hit' this holiday season as shipping costs spike

When Jenni Shields began her small business eight years ago selling handmade hair accessories, she paid $2.93 to ship an order, or close to $4 in today's dollars when adjusted for inflation.

Last year, Shields said, an average shipping label for her products cost around $4.50. But when she took a typical batch of orders to her local U.S. Postal Service office last month in Kansas City, Kansas, she was shocked to see that it was an average of nearly $6 per package.

Shields is just one of the small- and medium-sized retailers who are struggling to keep up with rising shipping costs as the price of fuel continues to trend upward.

Small business owners such as Shields are facing a difficult holiday season. Customer demand is strong, but some retailers said their profits will be dented — if not eaten up entirely — by higher shipping and other costs.

Shields said she charges her customers $6 in shipping fees, which includes roughly $1.50 to cover her packaging supplies. The dramatic upswing this year means she's no longer breaking even on her orders and instead eating the added costs, she said.

"I am losing money at this point, and I've really taken a huge hit this year," said Shields, the owner of Evie Pearl Handmade, which sells bows, quilted headbands and other accessories. "I feel like it's just been this slow increase up [in shipping costs], and I feel horrible."

The surge in fuel prices since the U.S. and Israel attacked Iran in February has driven up the cost of shipping by sea, air, rail and road.

That means higher costs across the board, including for shipping giant Amazon, which reported an 11% increase in shipping expenses in the third quarter compared with last year, to individual retailers.

Globally, air cargo is going for about $3.37 per kilogram, up 43% from a similar period a year ago, according to transportation marketplace Freightos. Judah Levine, Freightos' head of research, said in addition to higher fuel, demand for fast shipping of hardware used to build data centers is helping to boost air freight rates, even weeks before peak last-minute holiday packages take to the skies.

For businesses, that runup is going to "squeeze their margins in the near-term," said Jason Seidl, managing director and transportation analyst at TD Cowen. "Larger companies tend not to be exposed to the spot market, and they'll have what we call dedicated deals in place."

But that doesn't help smaller companies, which are walking a delicate line of not wanting to alienate customers who have enjoyed free shipping for nearly a generation.

Todd Shelton, founder of the eponymous 23-year-old New Jersey clothing company that makes high-end men's jeans, T-shirts and button-up shirts locally, said he started charging customers for return shipping this week for the first time after absorbing the higher expenses this year. The shipping is deducted from customers' refunds.

Shelton said his costs have been rising for materials, too, such as his imported Japanese denim and shirt fabric from Portugal. The company is still profitable, but the higher costs have eaten into profit margins as they stay elevated, he's considering raising prices or charging for shipping to customers, he said.

Shields said she's considering raising her shipping fees for the first time in six years to keep up with the increases.

The holiday season is one of Shields' busiest. But she said she's seen a slowdown in sales because of shipping fees.

"I can't compete with Amazon on the free shipping. I can't do that. I can't afford that," she said. "I'm seeing so much of my percentage that's supposed to come back to me and my family being put towards the shipping costs."

With no sign of fuel prices declining significantly, Shields, who runs her business out of her spare bedroom at home, said she's not sure that small businesses will be able to emerge on the other side of the holidays.

Shippers including UPS, FedEx and USPS have all instituted holiday surcharges, with prices varying by package type and lasting through the end of the season. Though that's typical during the peak holiday period each year, those charges have continued to rise over the past few years.

But even as costs everywhere climb, consumers are still spending.

According to the National Retail Federation, retail sales through the end of the year are expected to grow 4.2%, translating to total spending of $1.01 trillion. That's compared with last year's holiday sales totaling $972 billion, which was 3.1% growth.

Some of that growth is from inflation, meaning consumers are simply paying more for the same goods.

"Customers all expect to spend more this holiday, but part of why they expect to spend more is they expect things to cost more, not because they'd like to spend more or they were hoping to get more quantity of stuff," said Aaron Cheris, the global retail practice head at Bain & Company. "So what I suspect happens is that non-discretionary categories do fine, because you buy the food you were going to need to buy anyway, but then the amount of cash that's left over for [discretionary] goods is less."

Still, Cheris said it's shaping up to be a record year for shipping costs, which is putting immense pressure on smaller retailers. That means many retailers may find themselves in financial distress in the first quarter of next year as they struggle to make it through the holiday season.

He added that larger retail companies will also have to reckon with rising costs, though they may not feel those effects until later quarters because they have shipping contracts that have already been locked in and have control over their own delivery fleets.

"If anything, it benefits the scale players over the little players, but it's kind of a little bit bad for everybody," Cheris said. "I think that is the challenge."

#144. Berkshire Hathaway more than doubles buybacks in third quarter as earnings decline

Berkshire Hathaway said Saturday it accelerated its share repurchases during the third quarter, as operating and overall profits took a hit.

The Greg Abel-led conglomerate bought back $9.6 billion worth of Berkshire stock. That's more than double the $4.5 billion repurchased in the second quarter and brings the company's total buybacks for 2026 to about $14.4 billion.

The sharp increase came as Berkshire's operating earnings — those from wholly-owned businesses — fell year over year, as profits from its insurance business pulled back.

Berkshire operating profits totaled $12.705 billion, down 5.8% from $13.485 billion in the year-earlier period. Insurance underwriting tumbled 43% year on year to $1.352 billion, while insurance investment income declined 14% to $2.734 billion. Berkshire's railroad, energy and manufacturing businesses all saw earnings grow year over year.

The company's overall earnings, which include Berkshire's sprawling stock market portfolio, also dropped 28% in the third quarter to $22.112 billion from $30.796 billion a year prior. Investment gains for the company fell nearly 50% to $9.407 billion.

Despite the earnings pullback, Berkshire stock has outperformed recently amid a massive rotation out of technology stocks. Class A shares are up more than 12% over the past month, while the S&P 500 has dropped 5.5% in that time. Year to date, Berkshire has gained 13%, while the broad market index is up just 7.8%.

Berkshire's five largest equity holdings remained the same in the third quarter: Alphabet, American Express, Apple, Bank of America and Coca-Cola.

Berkshire's cash hoard also grew 3% in the third quarter $377.435 billion from $365.514 billion in the second quarter. However, excluding rail and utility cash along with T-bill payables, the conglomerate's cash holdings totaled $352.3 billion, down 1.9% quarter over quarter.

— CNBC's Alex Crippen contributed reporting.

#142. Elon Musk appears to signal the end of the road for Cybertruck

Tesla will wind down production of its Cybertruck vehicles at its Texas factory next year, Elon Musk says.

He did not spell out whether it plans to stop making them entirely or shift production elsewhere, but did say the decision was to "make room" for building more of its self-driving Cybercab cars.

But the project's lead engineer, Wes Morrill, appeared to suggest the vehicle was being discontinued, writing on X: "Cybertruck was an incredible technology development platform and lives on through Cybercab."

With its distinctive angular looks, the Cybertruck attracted diehard fans, but was beset by delays before being launched in 2023 and has had disappointing sales numbers since.

Tesla fans have been mourning the news online, with one person calling it "the best vehicle Tesla has ever made, external" as he lamented it being "discontinued next year".

Musk responded to the user, external with the word "true", adding: "But the Cybertruck fleet will be around for a long time."

Tesla's stock rose 4.7% on Friday after his announcement.

The vehicle has always been a passion project for Musk. He said it was "way more awesome than people realise, external" in March, and called it the "best product Tesla has ever made to date, external" in April.

But there have always been big questions about whether consumers shared that view and whether the Cybertruck could cut through and earn a spot in the US' lucrative truck market.

It was not helped by inauspicious introduction to the public in 2019 when Musk attempted to show off the Cybertruck's apparently unbreakable glass, only for it to shatter during a live demonstration.

Despite the gaffe, the Cybertruck ended up receiving a 146,000 orders, Musk said in 2019, while it was still in development.

In the end, the vehicle launched in 2023, keeping its novel design - an angular shape with a body made of stainless steel.

In 2025, Tesla sold just 20,237 Cybertrucks, a decline from 38,965 a year earlier, according to figures from Kelley Blue Book.

Musk had predicted annual sales of 250,000 after commercial production of Cybertruck began in 2023.

Tesla fans praised its retro-futuristic look, which some said looked like something "straight out of Blade Runner, external".

But critics were far less kind, joking about its appearance and labelling it "ugly as sin, external".

Several recalls - including in 2025 affecting more than 46,000 Cybertrucks over concerns part of its trim could fall off - would not have helped.

"We need to make it at a price that people can afford," Musk told Wall Street analysts that year - which ended up being $61,000 (then Â£48,000) for all the "bells and whistles".

In 2026, the so-called "Cyberbeast" model comes in at $102,000.

Will Tesla's cybertruck recover from its shattering start?

Tesla truck has 150,000 orders despite launch gaffe

#141. Amazon's Astro home robot has fans but looks destined for the 'trash heap.' Why it's trying again

Jeff Bezos made a bold bet in 2017. A sci-fi and robotics enthusiast, Bezos was convinced that consumers would have a robot in their home in the not-too-distant future.

The Amazon founder and then-CEO told a group of the company's top device executives to get to work on building one, and within a few years, the team came up with the $1,450 Astro.

The public got its first look at Astro, essentially an Alexa-equipped tablet on wheels that can autonomously navigate a home, recognize faces and act as a roving security monitor, when it rolled on stage at Amazon's fall 2021 event.

But it never evolved beyond an invite-only product. Astro shipped to just a few thousand customers, some of whom now believe the robot's days are numbered. The company also released an Astro for businesses in 2023, before sunsetting it less than a year later.

Amazon isn't giving up on Bezos' dream of a household robot just yet, despite its stumbles with Astro.

Over the past several months, the company has assembled a new team that's working on a "new-to-world robot product," according to a CNBC analysis of job posts.

"Robots are about to step out of the movies and into our homes," a recent job listing for an engineer, which has since been removed, stated. The post details a product capable of navigating "dynamic home environments" and "autonomous operation alongside family members."

Amazon is known for making long-term bets at the risk of "being misunderstood" in the short term if it gives the company an early advantage in new markets, like it did with the Alexa voice assistant or Kindle e-reader.

CEO Andy Jassy reminded investors of this approach in his latest annual letter, where he said artificial intelligence and robotics were among the "biggest inflections of our lifetime."

"We try to anticipate what will make customers' lives easier and better every day, and invent the next inflection," Jassy wrote.

Analysts also expect the humanoid market to surge in the coming years. Barclays forecast in a January report that the global market, which it currently values at $2 billion to $3 billion, could reach roughly $40 billion by 2035, or as high as $200 billion "under the most optimistic scenarios."

Earlier this year, Amazon redeployed much of the team behind Astro and brought in an MIT roboticist to work on the home robot project, LinkedIn profiles show.

It also made a splashy acquisition to supercharge the effort.

In March, Amazon scooped up New York-based Fauna Robotics, which has built a 3.5-foot-tall humanoid called Sprout. At the time, the company said Fauna would become part of a new division dubbed the Personal Robotics Group.

Some Personal Robotics Group staffers previously worked in Amazon's Lab126 R&D unit, which has been behind many of Amazon's most successful consumer hardware products, like the Kindle e-reader, Echo smart speakers, Fire TV and Fire Tablet. Lab126 was also behind one of Amazon's biggest flops, the Fire Phone.

It's unclear what kind of home robot the group is developing, or the types of tasks it could handle, but a June job posting hinted at an effort to "create robots that are not just functional, but delightful."

Amazon could be looking to adapt Fauna's robots for homes or businesses. The Sprout humanoid, which was designed to be "friendly and capable" with expressive yellow eyebrows, has primarily been used by developers, as well as commercial and academic researchers.

An Amazon job posting published online in October describes a "Field Support Engineer" who can assist with customer support and on-site repair, while another listing seeks a user experience designer to "define what it feels like to live with a Fauna robot."

"Our goal is simple: make robots people actually want to live and interact with in everyday human spaces," the listing states.

Amazon spokesperson Brad Glasser told CNBC in a statement that the company acquired Fauna because it's "committed to tackling the challenges that can unlock the personal robotics category."

"We're optimistic about a future where personal robots are helping people in meaningful ways in everyday life, and we intend to play an important role in making that future a reality," Glasser said.

Glasser noted that there remains a substantial gap between what's possible in a controlled lab and what's possible in settings like the living room or a lobby. Amazon is working with the robotics community to solve these challenges, he said.

Emily Lugo Velez has been living with the Astro in her North Carolina home since 2023. Lugo Velez, who worked in IT and now runs an AI advisory business, said she and her husband weren't fazed by the robot's $1,000-plus price tag and quickly joined the waitlist.

"We spent so much money on this thing," Lugo Velez, 52, said in an interview. "I feel like we're just stuck with it, and we'll let it run around the house until it eventually dies, then send it to the trash heap."

She said she still uses the device to check on her pets and appliances when she's out of the house. But beyond that, Astro has primarily become a party trick and a "trip hazard."

Most days, she tells Astro to go back to its charging dock.

Executives who worked on Astro had discussed making it more capable by attaching an arm to pick up and inspect items without breaking them, said Ken Washington, who served as Amazon's VP of consumer robotics until 2023.

He told CNBC that he pushed for a bipedal robot, but leadership disagreed.

"We had Jeff [Bezos] in for show and tells and reviews, and I watched his body language pretty closely," Washington said. "I think he was disappointed with the lack of functionality, but I think he was impressed with its ability to map and navigate an undefined space under all lighting conditions."

For all the struggles with Astro, Amazon has a rich history in robotics.

The company acquired Kiva Systems for $775 million in 2012, which introduced mobile robots to its vast fulfillment network. Since then, Amazon's warehouse automation efforts have ballooned to over one million robots across its facilities that can pick, sort and ferry items to workers.

But homes offer unique obstacles for robots to navigate.

"The home is a completely different space," said Jeff Burnstein, president of the trade group Association for Advancing Automation, who has been in the robotics industry for 45 years. "Children, pets, grandparents, stairways. Nobody's home is set up exactly the same, so it's a much more challenging environment for a robot, certainly a robot that's walking."

Amazon may have more luck its second time around because the tools for building robots have advanced by "leaps and bounds" over the past few years, said Colin Angle, a co-founder and former CEO of Roomba maker iRobot.

Amazon attempted to acquire iRobot in 2022 before walking away from the deal two years later amid growing regulatory pushback.

"The toolkit that the Astro team had when they did it was clubs with nails in them, just incredibly primitive," Angle said. "It's difficult to understate just how much the robot industry has changed in the last two years."

Advances in AI models, sensors, battery systems and key mechanical components like actuators have spurred a new wave of development around robots for industrial and consumer use.

Elon Musk's Tesla has been developing its bipedal Optimus robots since 2021. Startups including Apptronik, Boston Dynamics, China's Unitree, Figure and 1X are also building humanoids. Agility Robotics, the company behind the bipedal Digit robot that counts Amazon as one of its backers, went public via a SPAC on Thursday.

Venture capital firms poured $33.4 billion into robotics and physical AI companies in the first half of the year, compared with $28.7 billion for all of 2025, according to PitchBook data.

Most humanoids are being developed with the manufacturing, logistics and healthcare industries in mind, but a growing group of companies are attempting to build robots for the home.

Apple is reportedly working on several home robot projects, including a tabletop device that resembles the Pixar lamp. Hugging Face, which Nvidia agreed to acquire for almost $13 billion in September, released the $399 Microduck in August. Angle, in May, launched a startup building fuzzy companion robots called Familiar Machines & Magic. Companies including Sunday, Tau and Nori claim their robots can help with household chores like folding laundry and clearing the table, but they remain mostly prototype or niche devices.

"[Astro] was so ahead of its time," Washington said. "It was such a big, massive leap that I felt like we had the right to own the home."

The robot's limited functionality and its $1,000-plus price tag likely made it a tough sell for many consumers, said a former Amazon employee who worked on Astro, who asked not to be named in order to speak frankly about the project.

"Our poor little Astro was around $2,000 when it came out, and that's a lot," the ex-employee said. "That's a lot for a consumer, and they want a robot that does a lot."

Amazon takes a long term view on new markets like personal robotics, and values what can be gained by being an early entrant, Glasser said.

Astro was designed to help Amazon build competencies and focus its ambition, he added. The company has applied many of the learnings, talent and experience from Astro into its other robotics efforts, Glasser said.

In Facebook groups for Astro owners, users have complained of diminished customer service and griped that Amazon's AIexa+ AI assistant isn't supported on Astro.

Some users have interpreted this to mean Astro could be discontinued. Others have tried to dump their units at a discount on eBay and Facebook Marketplace.

Glasser said Amazon continues to support Astro customers.

Joe Martin, an Astro owner who lives near Tucson, Arizona, said he feels he's gotten his money's worth out of the device over the past four years. He'd welcome another robot into his home if it could do more, like helping out around the house.

"Astro was $1,000 and it was pretty limited, but if it could bring a drink to me or dust furniture, I'd be intrigued by that," Martin, 65, said. "I think that's right around the corner."

The behavioural impact of a small incentive to invest in stocks-and-shares Isas could be huge

#139. Why smoky bacon crisps threaten to reignite rows over EU rules

The naming of smoky bacon-flavoured crisps has become the latest political headache to emerge from the government's bid for closer ties with the EU.

The government is negotiating a deal on food standards in a bid to cut costs for British companies trading with Europe.

Labour ministers have been keen to talk up the potential economic benefits of the agreement, which could be announced at a summit later this month.

But the deal's requirement for Britain to adopt EU food rules in a wide range of areas has threatened to reignite sovereignty debates from the Brexit era.

Labour ministers have been keen to sidestep talking about the impact of alignment in particular sectors, instead stressing the overall economic boost it expects the food deal to bring.

On Thursday, Environment Secretary Angela Eagle told a radio presenter he should "get a life" when he asked about the potential impact of the new rules on snacks, such as smoky bacon crisps.

But it highlights the political dangers for the government of realigning with regulations that British politicians have not been able to shape, with the Conservatives and Reform UK keen to pounce on cases showing Britain to be a "rule taker" from Brussels.

It is not the first regulatory skirmish over smoky bacon crisps - following previous headlines over how the well-loved British flavour could be affected by an EU decision in 2024 to phase out a number of artificial smoke flavourings.

Now, the flavour name itself has been called into question by a separate EU attempt to restrict how descriptions of meat are used to market food.

Under rules adopted this summer, the EU plans to restrict the use of 31 terms, including bacon, drumstick and steak, to products that contain meat, claiming consumers could be confused by plant-based alternatives using the same names.

The European snack industry is seeking clarity on how the rules, which will also cover terms such as pork, chicken and beef, would apply to meat-flavoured crisps, including those flavoured without the use of any meat extracts.

The UK government has said it expects the new regulations to be in scope of its proposed food standards deal.

This means they would also apply to companies marketing their products in Britain, which is among the biggest consumers of crisps in Europe, according to analysts Euromonitor., external

It has raised the prospect that crisps with names such as smoky bacon and roast chicken could have to be relabelled to be sold in British shops, although much remains unclear about how the rules will apply in practice.

The new EU rules, which will not come into force until after August 2029, will allow the European Commission to create exemptions for meaty names with an established long-term use to describe other types of food product.

The Brussels-based body is yet to set out how it plans to use this power, or what it means for snacks.

A group of conservative German MEPs are also seeking a specific carve-out for meat names in snacks and sauces in separate legislation, external currently making its way through the European Parliament.

The Food and Drink Federation, which represents British crispmakers including Walkers and KP Snacks, has been supportive of the government's bid to reach a food deal due to its potential to reduce trade barriers with Europe.

But it has highlighted the new rules as an example of where it wants the government to work with industry to ensure UK interests are reflected in EU decision making.

"Meat protections are an example of a new EU rule that UK businesses will need to comply with, but which the UK was not involved in developing," added the body's chief scientific officer Kate Halliwell.

The Potato Processors' Association, which also represents a number of major snackmakers, said it was monitoring the situation closely and wanted to see clarification of how the rules could affect its members.

Vanessa Richardson, its deputy director general, said her association supported moves to reduce "unnecessary barriers" in the trade of food products.

But she added it was important that the government's deal is implemented in a way that "avoids unintended consequences for established product descriptions which consumers clearly understand as referring to a flavour".

The Department for Environment, Food & Rural Affairs (Defra) says it stands ready to help British snack businesses prepare for the impact of the new EU rules.

It is not yet clear what a British version of the rules would look like, or whether they would need to apply from 2029 alongside the new European regulations.

#138. How does it affect me if share prices fall?

Major tech firms have seen their stock values fall after a sell-off by investors following the US midterm elections.

The fall in the value of their shares across the world has been blamed on an expectation of a slowdown in AI development, although it comes after sharp rises over recent years.

As companies grow, they may issue shares. Many of the largest companies in the UK have shares which are bought and sold on the London Stock Exchange.

Their collective performance is often quoted amid a blizzard of numbers that may feel confusing and irrelevant.

Booms or falls in the stock market make the news, but rarely does anyone mention the FTSE 100 during a coffee with friends.

Yet, there are good reasons why this performance affects your life and finances.

Many people's initial reaction to "the markets" is that they are not directly affected, because they do not invest money.

Yet there are millions of people with a pension - either private or through work - who will see their savings (in what is known as a defined contribution pension) invested by pension schemes. The value of their savings pot is influenced by the performance of these investments.

Pension savers mostly let experts choose where to invest this money to help it grow. Widespread falls in share prices are likely to be bad news for pension savers.

Hundreds of billions of pounds are held in defined contribution pensions at the moment.

So big rises or falls can affect your pension, but the advice is to remember that pension savings, like any investments, are usually a long-term bet.

Experts say that investors have always had to ride economic shocks. Investments, by definition, require a long-term outlook and strategy. So, they are urging people not to panic in such circumstances or make knee-jerk decisions.

Some people have a pension which promises a specific value, depending on their salary. Others have no pension at all.

Millions of people have been automatically enrolled into a pension and may have not really noticed. Those schemes see employers divert wages into a pension and contribute some money themselves. The government adds a little in tax relief.

In every case, the value of these pension savings is affected by investment performance. So "the markets" matter - maybe not as much as everyday wages, but for future pensions.

Timing is more critical for those at retirement age, as this may be when a retiree uses their pension pot to buy a retirement income, or annuity. The bigger the pot, the more income they will get in retirement.

As you approach retirement age, pension pots tend to be moved to less risky investments, such as government bonds. When stock markets fall, these bonds can do better.

Anyone who has a pension pot invested and is taking an income from it will again see their investment go up and down with the stock markets.

That could mean getting less than you expected if you cash in too much after stock markets have fallen, making it important to plan how to make up any of this shortfall, experts say.

However, higher interest rates tend to mean better rates offered on annuities, and there is a chance of more rate rises in the UK by the Bank of England.

If share prices fall for an extended period then that could have an effect on jobs.

That's because investors in the company expect a return on that investment.

When it falls for a while, then they would expect the business to address issues, for example by cutting costs, and therefore jobs.

There is a huge element of the unknown here though. Companies have to consider lots of factors when making decisions on investment and employment.

In purely investment terms, lower share prices can offer an opportunity to buy, in the hope that over the long term, they recover and rise. Many people will do this initially through a stocks and shares Individual Savings Account (Isa).

However, it is worth noting that the sell-off in tech stocks in particular came after big rises, so long-term investors are still likely to have seen gains.

Experts and regulators are at pains to point out that investments can go down as well as up, and urge people not to put everything into one investment, but to diversify.

Some people invest money in what are known as tracker funds. These go up and down in line with the performance of a certain index, such as the FTSE 100.

So if the index falls, so does the value of their investments and vice versa. One advantage of these funds is that they often cost relatively little to sign up to.

Yes, and millions of people do, but experts stress that a long-term strategy is required to ride out short-term falls. It is also important to ensure cash savings are held too, so unexpected expenses - like the need for a replacement boiler - can be covered.

The government is keen to get people in the UK to invest more, partly because it says that will help UK economic growth and partly because it is much more popular among individuals in the US and some parts of Europe.

It backed an advertising campaign to encourage people to invest, funded by some of the UK's biggest financial services firms.

But the choice of "Savvy the Squirrel" to front the campaign has raised some eyebrows. Some question whether the push could get anywhere close to the success of the Tell Sid adverts of the 1980s, which encouraged people to invest in the newly privatised British Gas.

When we see big shifts in the stock markets there can also be fluctuations in the value of currencies and exchange rates.

As a result, prices could go up, but could also go down.

Upheaval to economies across the world, to oil prices and so on, have a big impact on what we pay at home for goods and services, so there are lots of factors in play.

Are you concerned about the impact of tariffs on your business or personal finances? Do you have any questions regarding tariffs?

Clarification 19 December: This article was amended to provide more context around how companies issue shares.

#135. Why the US midterms sparked an AI stock selloff

Is the artificial intelligence (AI) gold rush coming to an end, or is this simply a reality check?

That's the question which is hanging over investors, and which has prompted steep falls on the world's stock markets.

The sell-off in the US on Thursday was most dramatic on the Nasdaq, home to tech industry titans such as Alphabet, Nvidia, Meta and Apple.

It followed sweeping gains by the Democrats in this week's midterm elections in the US, which have given the party full control of Congress.

A number of leading Democrats campaigned against the unhindered expansion of data centres, which have underpinned the growth of artificial intelligence.

Fears this will lead to more regulation of AI appear to have concerned investors. But worries about the industry's prospects have been growing for some time.

The release of Chat GPT by Open AI in late 2022 is widely seen as having generated massive public and corporate interest in the practical uses of AI. It also sparked a modern-day gold rush.

Investors flocked to put their money into companies producing computer chips and networking infrastructure, building data centres and providing the energy to power them, as well as developing the AI itself. The value of tech companies soared.

The boom continued into this year. Investments in AI are expected to reach $1trillion in 2026 alone according to research by Goldman Sachs. The sector has scooped up so much available cash that other industries have seen their own borrowing costs increase as a result.

But investors want returns, and in recent months concerns have been growing about whether the AI boom can produce the expected riches.

Those worries have emerged at a time when public comments from AI researchers casting doubt over the safety of artificial intelligence - even suggesting it could represent an existential threat to humanity - have been grabbing headlines and fuelling speculation that a clampdown could lie ahead.

US tech stocks edge back up after AI slowdown fears sent value of firms plummeting

How does it affect me if share prices fall?

These firms you've never heard of are feeding fears the AI bubble could burst

While the Trump administration has been all-in on AI, or "super intelligence" as the president likes to call it, many leading Democrats tapped into grassroots concerns about the expansion of data centres and their impact on local communities.

These were largely focused on the facilities' high consumption of water and electricity, which many feared would push up their household bills â as well as the noise, use of land, and general disruption associated with them.

A Gallup poll published in May showed 71% of Americans would oppose the building of a data centre in their community. The figure for a nuclear power station was 53%.

James Talarico, who won the Senate race in once staunchly Republican Texas, was one of those to respond to this scepticism.

"While we want to protect America's leadership in artificial intelligence, we are still grappling with how it will affect everything from how we work to how our children learn," he said.

"We must protect Americans and establish clear, consistent laws for businesses."

With control of both houses of Congress, Democrats are now expected to challenge the president's technology agenda more vigorously, while introducing legislation which could curb the expansion of the industry.

Texas' Democratic governor-elect Gina Hinojosa campaigned against data centres, pledging a moratorium on the construction of new facilities.

Hinojosa's win could present opposition to tech multi-billionaire Elon Musk, who has moved large parts of SpaceX and Tesla's operations to the state, for years a Republican stronghold.

The Tesla boss had spent nearly $450m to back Republican campaigns in the midterms.

As the co-founder of the British-based technology firm Arm Holdings, Tudor Brown, points out, "stock values are based on future earnings not present earnings". If the rewards are not likely to be as great as expected, some investors will choose to sell up.

The falls seen on stock markets over the past 24 hours would appear to have been triggered by the midterm results. But concerns that tech firms have become overvalued were being expressed long before the elections.

The question now is whether this is simply the removal of froth from an over-exuberant market, or a sign that the AI rush is a bubble about to burst, with much wider consequences.

Nandini Roy Choudhury of Future Market Insights thinks investors will assess whether the political shift could result in restrictions on data centre construction, energy consumption and the deployment of AI technology.

She adds there is unlikely to be an immediate widespread recovery in tech stocks as traders are likely to be more selective, favouring firms with stronger balance sheets.

Brown thinks the impact will be limited. Share valuations, he thinks, were inflated by expectations of "almost infinite AI growth", which have been followed by "a bit of a panic".

"There is a natural floor to this", he says. "After that it will reflect I think that actually there's still a lot of money being made in these companies".

#132. He spent $50,000 on Labubus but now the craze is dying

Ensito, with his custom-made Labubu teeth, posing with a new doll in his collection

For years, Labubu dolls seemed unstoppable.

Collectors young and old energetically snapped up "blind boxes" - sealed, mystery packages containing the quirky monsters - often paying $15 (Â£11) or more.

Those unable to get the characters they wanted were often prepared to pay much more on the resale market.

The hype around the creatures convinced a mega-fan from North Carolina, who goes by the name Ensito, to leave his tech job in 2024 to create Labubu content full-time, travelling as far as Italy and Spain to seek out the latest releases.

Ensito has spent more than $50,000 (Â£37,000) to build his collection of more than 400 Labubus, a figure he tells the BBC he's "embarrassed to say".

He even had a row of custom Labubu-like jagged teeth made by a dentist to wear at events.

But in recent months, Ensito's once-viral posts about the furry monsters have struggled. "Those videos used to be able to hit 10,000 really quickly but I'm struggling to do that now."

Ensito's focus has shifted to other trends like Hello Kitty and squishies.

It's a sign that the craze which made parent company Pop Mart worth more than US toy giants Hasbro and Mattel put together may be running out of steam. Analysts believe the Beijing-based brand is struggling to capture excitement beyond a single viral moment.

Pop Mart recently reported that global sales in the three months to the end of September had fallen by as much as 50% on the previous year.

The drop was particularly steep outside mainland China, with US revenue down by at least 70%.

Pop Mart didn't invent blind boxes but its dolls certainly helped to popularise them around the world.

The sales tactic made collecting feel "nostalgic", evoking the feeling of opening gifts as a child, says marketing professional Katelynn Carlson, who discovered Labubus through social media and now owns 36 of them.

One of her first Labubus was a rare doll, making the experience "even more exciting", she adds.

Labubu, which launched in 2019, has been Pop Mart's most successful line of toys. The monsters became fashion statements and were sported by celebrities including Kim Kardashian and Blackpink's Lisa.

They regularly attracted long queues and became so popular that Pop Mart had to temporarily pull the toys from its UK shops after reports of customers fighting over them. One fan paid more than $150,000 for a human-sized Labubu.

A key part of the allure was how difficult some Labubu dolls were to find, with fans repeatedly buying blind boxes to get the version they wanted, says National University of Singapore marketing lecturer Samer El Hajjar.

Katelynn Carlson believes blind boxes were an exciting feature of the Labubu craze

Pop Mart now has 500 shops in more than 30 countries and 2,000 vending machines worldwide. The firm, which listed in Hong Kong in 2020, is worth nearly $24bn and remains one of the world's most valuable toymakers, despite its shares having fallen by more than 50% from last year's peak.

Investors worry its global success was just a viral moment rather than sustainable growth driven by long-term customer loyalty, El Hajjar says.

For Ensito, it is clear the dolls' exclusivity helped fuel the craze.

"Now it doesn't feel that way. I walk into a Pop Mart and there's a whole series of Labubus sitting there," he says.

Ashley Dudarenok of China research firm ChoZan says that sense of exclusivity faded as Pop Mart ramped up production to meet demand.

Pop Mart declined to comment on share price moves and did not respond to questions about falling sales.

As the Labubu mania cools, some of its rivals have become more popular.

Blind box collectables like Sonny Angels - tiny, unclothed cherub-like dolls - and miniature glow-in-the-dark Smiski figurines have been flying off shelves. And you can now find My Little Pony and KPop Demon Hunters in blind boxes too.

Miniso, founded in the southern Chinese city of Guangzhou, sells a range of blind-boxed Disney and Marvel characters in more than 8,000 shops globally.

"Consumers aren't particularly concerned about where the brand comes from," Miniso executive Vincent Huang said earlier this year. "They're more focused on the shopping experience - the designs, value for money, and enjoyment."

Pop Mart faces competition from retailers like Miniso

Experts say one of Pop Mart's biggest challenges is creating enduring characters.

That's an area where Hello Kitty brand Sanrio, which posted record earnings in the year to March, has a clear advantage, analyst Boustani suggests.

Pop Mart has invested heavily in ways to grow its brand, including setting up a theme park in Beijing and announcing a Labubu film - following in the footsteps of franchising giants like Disney or Lego, some experts believe.

But unlike Disney, which sells a story before it sells a toy, Pop Mart is working in reverse, says Dudarenok. "The real test for next year is whether Pop Mart can make people love a character without a celebrity holding it."

Glow-in-the-dark Smiskis are emerging as a competitor to Labubus

After spending huge sums on the monsters, collector Ensito says he is now questioning how much time and money people invest in collections each time a new trend comes along. "There's always going to be a trend every year. There's always gonna be something that's gonna be really popular."

Melbourne-based content creator Lawrence Yu says that after two years of chasing Labubus, he's likely to be "more selective" before adding to his roughly 50 dolls.

His Labubu shopping and unboxing clips once drew up to a million views but the frenzy has faded and Yu rarely makes videos about them now. "I definitely think the trend has slowed down... People go crazy and then it fizzles."

Clara Barbero from Canada believes Labubus are a "dead trend" but is still keeping an eye out for interesting releases.

"Iâm curating my collection more and only buying what I see myself liking for the next few years, and not just buying the newest series thatâs out."

Lawrence Yu says he has spent about $1,400 on Labubu dolls

But the fluffy monsters still have truly devoted fans.

Hedy Khaled from Liverpool says although the hype around the dolls is cooling, she is just as attached to her collection of 70 Pop Mart figurines.

There's a distinction between those who chase trends and "real fans", she argues, so falling sales do not necessarily mean Labubus have lost their appeal.

For Carlson says it is the happiness the dolls bring, not the viral trend, that keeps her collecting. She still gets excited about new Pop Mart releases, like its recent cat-themed Labubu series, which has become one of her favourites.

"I think there's something special about embracing our inner child and enjoying things simply because they make us happy."

'I've spent more than Â£3,000 on Jellycats'

'I have the world's biggest My Little Pony collection'

#130. Where's the growth? Legacy automakers face a tougher 2027 as costs rise, market tightens

DETROIT — There's a growing concern that the global automotive industry may be stuck in neutral, if not reverse, heading into 2027.

The auto industry — from the companies to their customers — has been surprisingly resilient this decade, but there are fears that rising costs, weaker vehicle pricing and a more challenging market could threaten profits and growth going forward.

Automakers also face pressure outside the U.S. from an onslaught of Chinese carmakers, a thinner new-product pipeline after retreating from some all-electric plans, and company- and region-specific challenges.

"With investor focus increasingly shifting to 2027, the setup appears challenging," Barclays analyst Dan Levy said in an investor note last month. "Rising inflation, continued China pressure, affordability concerns in the U.S., and a flat global production outlook suggest another year of limited earnings growth."

Several Wall Street analysts have said automakers will need continued pricing strength and additional cost savings to offset inflation, higher investment spending and a potentially weaker economy just to maintain their current levels.

"It's a little bit more optimistic in the U.S. and then very pessimistic in Europe," RBC Capital Markets analyst Tom Narayan told CNBC. "As a whole, there's some negativity on '27."

Automakers from the U.S. to Europe to Asia have been facing a growing list of headwinds.

For U.S.-based legacy automakers, Ford Motor said it expects next year's results to be in line with 2026, while General Motors grew more cautious about its ability to continue growing earnings as much as it had previously expected.

"We continue to believe that 2027 will be another strong year and better than 2026, assuming customer demand remains strong, and commodity, logistics and other recent cost pressures moderate," GM CEO Mary Barra told investors Oct. 20.

GM CFO Paul Jacobson said that while it's too early to officially forecast, the company is facing additional inflationary costs that could lead to another $2 billion headwind in 2027, like it expects this year. He specifically cited costs of commodity inflation, logistics and DRAM, or dynamic random-access memory chips as challenges.

Those chips are critical components in modern vehicles and are used for everything from temporary data storage and real-time processing from infotainment systems to safety-critical systems.

GM and others have been able to offset outside pressures through cost cuts, operational efficiencies and resilient consumer demand for higher-margin vehicles. However, there's a limit to what companies can do.

"There's only so much pricing you can push to consumers," Narayan said. "They're already at pretty high pricing."

European automakers including Volkswagen, Mercedes-Benz and BMW in recent weeks reduced their 2026 earnings forecasts in response to U.S. tariffs, the growth of Chinese automakers and rising costs.

Most recently, BMW this week said accelerated restructuring measures would reduce its automotive pretax earnings margin this year by 2.5 percentage points. The company said the restructuring should support "positive effects" into 2027 and a 3% to 5% pretax profit margin for 2028. That compares with a 5.3% automotive margin last year and 3.4% during the third quarter.

Chrysler parent Stellantis, which has large operations in Europe and the U.S., is facing its own challenges, as it's in the midst of a restructuring that aims for a low-single-digit adjusted operating margin this year. It has maintained a 2026 guidance that includes a mid-single-digit percentage increase in net revenue and a low-single-digit adjusted operating margin.

In Asia, South Korean automaker Hyundai Motor, which has experienced notable growth in recent years, also cut key metrics when it reported earnings late last month. The company lowered its 2026 wholesales target to 4 million vehicles, down from 4.16 million, and cut its operating-margin target to between 5.5% and 6.5%, down from a previous goal of 6.3% to 7.3%.

Toyota Motor is an outlier for its most recent quarter and outlook, as the world's largest automaker increased its full-year operating income forecast by 500 billion Japanese yen (about $3.2 billion) on Thursday.

Toyota's results were boosted by weakness in the Japanese yen as well as strong hybrid vehicle demand in the U.S. However, the increase in operating income was its first in six quarters.

Broadly, the changes to earnings and outlooks come as production for the global industry slows.

Consulting and insights firm GlobalData expects global light-vehicle production of about 90.96 million units in 2026, down 2.1% compared with last year. It forecast a production increase of less than 1% next year. That comes on the heels of strong annual growth earlier this decade, led by China.

Cox Automotive reported the average transaction price of a new U.S. vehicle in September was more than $50,500. That was just shy of the record of $50,614 in December 2025.

Lofty vehicle prices have led to record-high and record-long financing deals, as well as concerns that customers are getting priced out of the new-vehicle market.

The typical U.S. new-vehicle payment reached $776 a month in September, the highest since December 2023, according to Cox Automotive.

Cox Automotive chief economist Jeremy Robb, discussing the third quarter for the automotive industry, rated the overall economy, buyer ability and borrowing costs as cautionary "yellow lights."

But he did have some "green lights," such as good credit availability and employment conditions, which could give some relief for automakers and consumers, many of whom need vehicles for their everyday lives.

Robb said there's a "tug-of-war" between headwinds such as rising costs, interest rates and fuel prices and tailwinds such as growth in liquid assets and savings that has helped offset some of the additional costs.

"Consumers remain under financial pressure, but the vehicle demand continues to hold, showing more resiliency in the auto industry than we've seen in other parts of the economy," Robb said during a Sept. 24 call, noting consumer sentiment remains low.

#128. 'My mortgage is going up £200 a month': How rising interest rates are hitting people's pockets

The BBC spoke to three people about how they could be affected by rising interest rates

On Thursday, the Bank of England increased interest rates for the first time in over three years as it tries to get a grip on rising inflation linked to the Iran war.

Experts say there could be more hikes over the next 18 months, as the conflict continues to push up food, fuel and energy prices across the UK and worldwide.

Higher interest rates affect borrowing costs, however, which means mortgages, credit cards and personal loans could get more expensive for millions, even as savings rates improve.

For some, it could mean extra financial pressure at a time when they are already struggling with the cost of living. The BBC spoke to three people about how they could be affected.

Peter Gill says his family's costs are rising

Peter Gill, 48, is one of the around 1.3 million Britons on a variable rate mortgage.

These loans track the Bank of England base rate, with a margin on top determined by the lender, so any rise in interest rates can push up repayments.

Peter, who runs a small tiling business in Halifax, says his monthly repayments will go up by roughly Â£45 following Thursday's quarter-percentage point increase and he's worried they could climb higher.

But he still thinks it'd be more expensive to move onto a fixed-rate deal - which is when you pay a set amount of monthly interest for a period - and is prepared to stay put for now.

Watch: What does the raise in interest rates mean?

His higher mortgage costs come as the "cost of everything is going up", he says.

The father of two, who has a combined household income of Â£45,000 with his wife Faye, is having to pay his staff more because of increases in the national minimum wage. Raw material costs have also risen due to the war in Iran.

At home, the family's food, gas and electricity bills have gone up and he says he's working "about six and a half days a week" to shield them from the worst of it.

"From my point of view, inflation goes up which costs you, then interest rates go up and you have even less money."

Dawn Fazackerley-King is struggling but says there's "no more fat to trim"

Most UK mortgage holders are on fixed-rate deals, but that doesn't necessarily protect them when the Bank of England puts up rates.

David Hollingworth, from brokers L&C Mortgages, says many people will be shocked at how much higher fixed-rate deals are than they were five years ago following recent increases.

"Fixed rates have already risen due to market anticipation that interest rates would need to rise to combat higher inflation," he says.

Dawn Fazackerley-King is a Pilates instructor who lives near Ipswich and has previously spoken to the BBC about her struggles with the cost of living.

She was on a five-year fixed deal at 1.4%, which she took out in January 2022. But the mother of two is about to refix for two years and the best deal she can find is a 5.69% loan. That will push her costs up by about Â£200 a month.

"I don't know where that money is going to come from, it's something we are going to have to figure out as a family. It's stressful."

She and her husband, who have a joint income of about Â£70,000, have already gone through their direct debits and there's "no more obvious fat to trim".

There is also no scope for them to take on additional work, as they both work full time.

"We're still living in the same property, our household costs are remaining the same," Dawn says. "It's an additional cost of several hundred pounds but we're not getting anything extra for that."

Higher interest rates are "another cost to deal with", says Ben

When the Bank of England increases its base rate, credit card borrowing costs also tend to increase, although the impact is not always immediate.

It may affect people with outstanding debts, as providers can increase the interest rates they charge with 30 days' notice.

Ben Thomas, 41, from Shropshire, ditched his career as a chartered accountant during the Covid pandemic after a mental health crisis and retrained as a paramedic.

But the drop in income left him reliant on credit cards and personal loans for everyday living expenses and he is now Â£52,000 in debt.

Faisal Islam: How high will interest rates go?

Three ways the interest rate rise could affect you

Ben says he's reached an agreement to pause the interest on most of it, but he is still incurring charges on around Â£2,000 of credit card debt and is worried about his repayments going up.

"With all that's going on, rising interest rates is another cost to deal with," he tells the BBC.

As well as overall debt repayments of around Â£300 a month, he and his wife pay Â£760 on their mortgage. They have also struggled with higher food and energy bills.

Simon Trevethick is head of communications at StepChange, which has been helping Ben manage his debt. He says the debt charity has seen a 20% increase in people asking for support this year and warned of tougher times ahead.

"If higher borrowing costs feed through into household budgets over the coming months, we could see further pressure on people seeking support."

Do you have a mortgage, a credit card, a car finance agreement? How are interest rates affecting you?

#127. Could supplements be the next big opportunity in the GLP-1 weight-loss drug boom?

Supplements could be the next major sector to benefit from the weight-loss drug boom, Novonesis CEO told CNBC on Monday, as the biosolutions company explores whether microbiome products can help patients on popular GLP-1 medicines.

"We have scientific evidence that [is] going to support the patients [on] GLP-1s feeling better, as a companion during the treatment," CEO Ester Baiget told "Squawk Box Europe," referencing an ongoing study with Novo Nordisk.

GLP-1 medications and dietary supplements, such as those being developed by Novonesis, could play complementary roles in managing obesity and supporting longer-term health, she argued.

While medications can help treat obesity, improving the gut microbiome through nutrition and scientifically backed supplements could support longer-term health, Baiget said.

The microbiome refers to the community of microorganisms, including bacteria, fungi and viruses, that live in or on different parts of the body, particularly in the gut.

Baiget also sees strong commercial demand for supplements with proven health benefits.

"We see that when we prove science with answers, there is a tremendous pull in the market," she said. Novonesis is currently studying what Baiget referred to as "a unique cocktail" of pro-, pre- and post-biotics to support patients taking GLP-1s made by Wegovy and Ozempic maker Novo Nordisk.

The lucrative weight-loss drugs market pioneered by Novo Nordisk and U.S. rival Eli Lilly has had far-reaching consequences beyond health companies.

From consumer staples and food companies reassessing meal composition and sizes, to fashion companies seeing a boost from consumers buying new clothes after dropping sizes, drugs like Wegovy and Zepbound are widely considered to be reshaping consumer spending across industries.

According to a PwC survey of U.K. adults, about 70% of GLP-1 users are spending less on snacks, while 40% are spending more on vitamins and supplements. More than 80% of respondents who had now stopped taking the medicine said they maintained some of the dietary changes they adopted while on treatment.

Meanwhile, the global dietary supplements market was valued at an estimated $209.5 billion in 2025 and is projected to more than double to $431.7 billion by 2033, according to Grand View Research.

Growing health awareness is driving demand for supplements and functional foods and drinks containing ingredients such as vitamins and probiotics, as consumers seek benefits beyond basic nutrition, the market research firm found.

The global probiotic supplements market was estimated to be worth around $10.5 billion in 2025, growing to $17.5 billion by 2033.

Some research has linked GLP-1 treatment to insufficient protein intake and deficiencies in certain vitamins and minerals, but the extent to which the medications themselves are responsible remains unclear. The microbiome-based products being studied by Novonesis are different from protein and vitamin supplements, and any potential benefit for GLP-1 users has yet to be established.

For Novonesis, if benefits are scientifically proven, it could be a welcome boost to its human health business, which is currently under pressure.

Its human health business reported an 8% decline in organic sales in the third quarter, weighed down by weaker demand for probiotic supplements sold through offline channels in North America.

The company, formed through the merger of Novozymes and Chr. Hansen in 2024, is a leader in enzymes and microbial solutions used across industries such as food, agriculture, and healthcare. Its products are used in everything from dairy fermentation to dietary supplements.

The company holds nearly 50% of the enzymes and cultures market, compared with around 20% for rival International Flavors & Fragrances, according to Morningstar.

Novonesis gets about a third of total sales from the North American market, while Human Health accounts for about 11% of its total sales.

#124. Government 'looking seriously' at banning under-16s from Vinted and eBay

Vinted and eBay could be included in the list of platforms set to be banned for children aged under 16, the culture secretary has said.

Lisa Nandy said the government was "looking seriously" at including the retailing websites in the social media ban "to keep children safe".

The UK's social media ban for under-16s is set to come into force in March next year, with children barred from accessing platforms such as Instagram, TikTok and Snapchat.

In response, eBay said it was looking forward to "understanding more and continuing to work constructively with the government". Vinted said it welcomed "open dialogue" with ministers and the regulator on the issue.

Asked on the BBC's Sunday with Laura Kuenssberg programme whether the government was looking to ban under-16s from Vinted and eBay, Nandy said: "We are looking seriously at doing so in order to keep children safe."

It came after the Sunday Times reported, external that the UK's social media ban was being expanded to include the popular second-hand online marketplaces.

The move was due to fears that messaging functions on the platforms allowed strangers to groom children, according to the newspaper. It suggested similar reselling platforms, Depop and Facebook Marketplace, would also be included in the list of banned sites.

Businesses and individuals sell a host of goods on eBay, which has been a major global player in the online reselling market for years. The platform states on its website that it currently requires all of its members to be over 18 years old because listings involve "entering into a contract".

Vinted is aimed at over 18s, but its terms and conditions do allow parents or guardians to create accounts for their children, which must be used under parental supervision.

The site, on which users mostly buy and sell unwanted clothes and other fashion items, has grown rapidly recently to reach 17 million UK users.

A Vinted spokesperson said direct messaging was "part of how the platform works" but that it had a "zero tolerance for harmful or explicit content" and had moderation teams and tools to spot and remove "activity that breaks our rules".

"When we identify inappropriate behaviour, we take action, up to a permanent ban or reporting to the relevant authorities," they said.

An eBay spokesperson said the company was "focused on providing a trusted and safe marketplace experience for our millions of customers".

"While the details of the proposed legislation are not yet clear, we look forward to understanding more and continuing to work constructively with the government," they added.

The government announced it would ban under-16s accessing social media in June this year, with children prevented from accessing 10 of the biggest platforms.

YouTube will also be banned for under-16s, but YouTube Kids - a version of the video-sharing app designed to be safe for children - will not be included in the measures.

A complete list of affected platforms has not yet been released, but the government said it would cover those "whose purpose is to enable social interaction and which allow users to post material".

That means platforms that have livestreaming or features that enable the potential for strangers to contact children will also be restricted, which includes gaming sites.

It does not mean that Roblox, the biggest gaming site in the UK for those aged eight to 12, for example, will be banned for under-16s. However, certain features such as its chat function will be restricted.

But Matthew Sinclair, a senior director at the Computer and Communications Industry Association, which represents tech companies, argued that ministers should prevent the rules having "scope creep".

He said a restriction on messaging for under-16s "would not stop at social media and would reach into the services families depend on to stay in touch", and urged the government to "ensure any new restrictions are proportionate and effective."

The UK's ban comes after a similar one was introduced in Australia last year.

Many children there have found ways to get round it, but the UK has said it intends to take a tougher approach.

In recent weeks, attention has turned to how a social media ban would be enforced.

Last month, the communications regulator Ofcom set out its recommendations for the most effective ways to identify which of the UK's millions of social media users are aged over 16.

Rani Govender, head of online child safety at the NSPCC, said the "real question" going forward was how the government ensured age checks were effective.

"But effective age checks are only part of the solution," she added. "Tech companies must also be required to build child safety into every platform, device and AI tool, so children are protected from harmful and illegal content and only able to access age-appropriate services."

Are you a parent? Do you have any views or questions on this story?

#121. Why tech workers are flocking to NYC

Erin Sullivan had only visited New York City once before she decided to take a job in the Big Apple.

But that visit made a strong impression: "Having the freedom of just hopping on the subway and getting anywhere was so magical for me," she says. "I knew that I wanted to end back up" in the city.

After graduating from the University of Oklahoma in 2023 with a computer science degree, Sullivan, 25, landed a role as a software engineer at Bank of America in New York City.

She's part of a growing contingent of tech workers who are drawn to the city.

The New York metro area is now home to the most tech workers in the country with 394,300 jobs, edging out the San Francisco Bay Area's 375,730 jobs, according to an August report from CBRE, a real estate services and investment firm.

Between 2022 and 2025, New York's pool of tech employees grew by over 30,000, while San Francisco's shrank by nearly 24,000 workers.

In today's tech talent market, "the Big Apple is on top," says Lauren Crowley Corrinet, vice chairman at CBRE.

But a big part of New York's tech hiring boom comes from outside the traditional tech sector, according to Julie Samuels, president and CEO of Tech:NYC, a nonprofit representing the city's technology industry.

According to Tech:NYC's data, just 35% of NYC's tech employees work at tech companies and startups, while 64% are employed by non-tech companies like banks and media organizations, based on their report published in November 2026.

"When you think about industries like finance, like real estate — industries that have been in New York for a really long time — it has become increasingly clear that technology is driving their work as well," Samuels says.

To keep up with artificial intelligence developments, "everyone is hiring" tech workers and consultants, she says: "All these traditional industries are thinking about how they can bring AI into the fold in their own work."

And AI companies are expanding their reach in the city, too: OpenAI leased a 90,000-square-foot office in lower Manhattan, and Anthropic says it plans to double its New York workforce to 1,000 employees in 2026.

It's no mystery why workers are drawn to New York City, according to Corrinet: "Everything's here, and it's just such a rich and engaging place for people to live."

But New York's high cost of living can pose a challenge even for tech workers "who make six figures right out of college," according to Samuels. On the other hand, there are valid concerns that an influx of highly paid tech workers could worsen the affordability crisis for other New Yorkers, she says.

Her goal is to ensure that New York's growing tech presence benefits all New Yorkers, not just those who work in the industry.

Watch the video above to hear more from Samuels, Corrinet and Sullivan — plus more tech workers who are choosing NYC.

#120. Nvidia launches investigation into CEO's affair with suspected Chinese spy

Jensen Huang admitted having an extramarital affair and said he would cooperate into a security investigation launched by the tech giant

US tech giant Nvidia has opened an independent investigation following reports its CEO Jensen Huang had a relationship with a suspected Chinese spy.

Nvidia, one of the most valuable companies in the world, said its board had no reason to believe the company's secrets or national security were compromised, adding that the CEO was committed to continuing to lead the organisation.

Separately, Jensen Huang admitted to having been "unfaithful" to his wife and apologised for hurting her and their children.

US intelligence officials believed that Chinese businesswoman Alice Huang, who had a close relationship with Jensen Huang, was a spy for Beijing, according to a New York Times report., external

Jensen Huang said he took "full responsibility" for what he did, adding that he had fallen short of his responsibilities to Nvidia's employees, board and shareholders.

Nvidia is one of the world's largest companies and seen as central to making the hardware for emerging AI technologies, and Jensen Huang has been in charge of it for more than 30 years.

He said: "I was unfaithful to my wife. My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did. I am deeply sorry."

His statement continued: "Regarding the national security concerns raised in news reports, Nvidia's Board of Directors has initiated an independent investigation. I support the investigation and will cooperate fully.

"I love our country and our company. I am committed to protecting Nvidia and supporting America's national security. I am proud and grateful to be an American."

A separate statement issued by Nvidia's board of directors said it would begin an investigation in "light of recent reporting that founder and CEO Jensen Huang has been the target of a foreign intelligence effort".

It continued: "The Board prioritises US national and economic security, which is paramount to the interests of Nvidia and its shareholders.

"While the Board has no reason to believe that either national security or Nvidia secrets were compromised, the Board's inquiry will examine all available information."

US President Donald Trump said he had not spoken to the Nvidia boss since news of the investigation emerged.

"I just know he's a fine person, but you never know what's going on," Trump told reporters, adding that he had "done an amazing job for our country".

The New York Times reported in late October that Trump had been briefed last year on intelligence alleging Alice Huang was spying on China's behalf.

Asked by reporters on Sunday whether he now wished he had "acted on the intelligence" at the time, Trump responded: "No, I don't think so. I think he's an honourable guy."

Secretary of State Marco Rubio, meanwhile, refused to comment when asked about Huang before flying to Paris for a meeting of the G7.

Rubio told the press: "I can't comment on any of that. I just can't comment because of the nature of it. And I also can't comment on an internal corporate matter that's going on with Nvidia, as you highlight, I don't know anything about that part of it so."

Last spring, the secretary of state reportedly oversaw the drafting of a memo that warned the Trump administration about Huang's relationship with the Chinese businesswoman, according to the New York Times.

China has previously rejected allegations of an espionage operation targeting the Nvidia chief, describing the claims as "groundless speculation".

Foreign Ministry spokesperson Guo Jiakun told reporters at a daily press briefing: "We have already responded to this. The allegations about Chinese spies are entirely groundless, speculative and intended to smear and malign China."

Watch: Jensen Huang is a 'fine person' who has done 'an incredible job', says Trump

Jensen Huang rescued Nvidia from bankruptcy on more than one occasion, and he took risks on AI a long time before the rest of the world did.

His decision to make Nvidia chips, originally designed for gaming, become the best in the business for AI development, has now paid off in spades and the company is worth trillions.

This is a big blow for a man who is renowned for being a workaholic, a perfectionist and someone who expects incredibly high standards from his staff.

Alice Huang, who is of no relation to Jensen Huang, is linked to Singapore-based data centre firm Megaspeed.

The company told the New York Times last month its current leadership took the allegations seriously and had appointed external legal advisers to conduct an internal investigation.

Nvidia is a central player in AI infrastructure, providing chips to leading AI model developers including OpenAI and Meta.

The microchip manufacturer is worth some $4.5 trillion, making it one of the most valuable companies ever.

US intelligence agencies had been monitoring Alice Huang and concluded that she was acting on behalf of the Chinese government, according to unnamed sources cited by the New York Times.

Surveillance reportedly revealed communications between Alice Huang and an American citizen, who was subsequently identified as Jensen Huang through a process known as "unmasking".

It remains unclear what information Alice Huang â who is also known as Huang Le â allegedly passed to Beijing, how valuable it was, or when she is believed to have begun gathering intelligence.

Alice Huang did not respond to the paper's request for comment.

Last week, members of Congress wrote to Jensen Huang, asking him to appear before them in the light of the reported relationship.

Three House Republican committee leaders asked the CEO to appear later in the month, to talk about Nvidia's "security protocols against foreign adversary espionage" given his reported relationship with Alice Huang.

"Recent reports raise significant national security concerns regarding the PRC's [China's] access to sensitive US technology and information.

"The issue of PRC infiltration and access to cutting-edge US technology is core to the investigative work of our three committees this Congress," the letter said.

Nvidia's AI chips have been a major focus of the rivalry between the US and China, and Washington has previously imposed controls on where its advanced products can be sold.

The H200, Nvidia's second most advanced semiconductor, had previously been restricted by Washington over concerns that it would give China's technology industry an edge over the US.

In January, the Trump administration said it would allow the company to sell its H200 chips to Chinese customers under certain conditions.

#118. Government's EV targets expected to be reduced this week

The government launched a review into its policy to ban new petrol and diesel car sales by 2035

The UK government's electric vehicle (EV) targets are expected to be reduced this week in a move likely to be welcomed by carmakers but opposed by environmental groups.

Under the current rules, 80% of new cars sold in the UK must be electric by 2030 but industry groups have long called for "urgent relief".

New petrol or diesel cars are due to be phased out by 2030 and by 2035 all new cars must be zero emission.

But the speed of the transition to electric has been hotly debated, including within the cabinet.

The government has been consulting on reducing its 80% rule and a significant reduction is now expected.

Downing Street has insisted no decision has been made.

A government source told the BBC it would approach the issue in a "pragmatic and balanced" way.

If ministers do decide on a steeper watering-down of the rules, it will arguably be interpreted by some - alongside the approval of the Jackdaw gas field in the North Sea - as the government prioritising economic considerations over environmental ones.

Prime Minister Andy Burnham has recused himself from this issue due to his wife's links to an electric vehicle company.

The key meetings on the subject will instead be chaired by Cabinet Office minister Louise Haigh.

Asked about reports the targets would be reduced, Burnham dismissed this as "speculation", adding: "No decision has been made at this point."

Sustainability groups say any weakening of the 2030 target will threaten the UK's long-term electrification and climate goals.

It could undermine the UK's claim to be providing leadership on climate just as ministers head to Turkey for COP31, the UN's climate summit.

Energy UK, the trade association for the energy industry, has also opposed the move.

Last month, it said, external reducing targets could damage "the investment certainty needed to deliver the infrastructure, innovation and services required for the transition to zero-emission transport".

Asked if climate change was no longer a big priority, Health Minister Karin Smyth said the government was committed to its emissions targets.

"This country is doing great work in terms of our transition and looking at renewables â that's a key part of growth in different parts of the country," she told BBC Breakfast.

She added that the prime minister would have "a good story to tell" when meeting other leaders at COP31.

A government spokesperson said: "We are committed to the phase out of all new cars relying solely on petrol and diesel engines by 2030, and to 100% of new car and van sales being zero emission by 2035, and our legally binding net zero targets."

The Society of Motor Manufacturers and Traders welcomed the government's review of the EV targets when it was announced in the summer.

Last week, head of the association Mike Hawes said transitioning to electric vehicles had come at a "heavy cost" with billions spent on discounts to drive demand in electric vehicles.

He said the government's targets could not be achieved "without putting at risk any future investment".

UK electric car sales target set to be weakened

Electric vehicle sales targets could be cut after pressure from car makers

EU waters down plans to end new petrol and diesel car sales by 2035

Net zero means balancing the amount of planet-warming "greenhouse" gases produced by human activities with the amount being actively removed from the atmosphere.

The government's policy to phase out petrol and diesel vehicle sales has undergone multiple changes since it was first introduced by former Prime Minister Boris Johnson in 2020.

His successor Rishi Sunak pushed back the timeline to 2035, and the Burnham government set up a review of the EV sales targets in August.

Sunak introduced phased targets for EV sales in the UK, known as the Zero Emission Vehicles (ZEV) mandate.

Under the ZEV mandate, the percentage of new car sales that need to be EVs increases each year. The target was 28% for 2025, 33% for 2026, and so on until it reaches 80% by 2030.

Pressure in the European Union saw the bloc scrap a similar plan to ban new petrol and diesel car sales by 2035, particularly from countries with large auto industries such as Germany.

The Conservative Party has also vowed to abandon the petrol car ban if it wins the next election, with leader Kemi Badenoch calling it a "well-meaning, but ultimately destructive piece of legislation".

Green Party leader Zack Polanski said the government was "building up a shocking record of backsliding and bad decisions on climate issues".
