#117. CoreWeave confronts 'doom cycle' narrative as neocloud credit risk overshadows growth

After a tumultuous month for neoclouds that saw stock prices cut in half, investors are eager to hear how the leading companies in the space plan to navigate a tightening credit market and an unfavorable political environment.

CoreWeave is set to report quarterly results on Tuesday, followed by Nebius on Thursday. Both companies are reliant on debt to fund their purchases of cutting-edge artificial intelligence systems, but they're confronting a skeptical base of lenders as the AI trade sours on Wall Street.

The neoclouds had a particularly rough day last Thursday, suffering dramatic drops and contributing to the Nasdaq's steepest slump in 19 months. Leading chipmakers Nvidia, Advanced Micro Devices and Micron also plummeted on concern that raging anti-AI sentiment would further complicate buildout plans.

Nvidia, a major investor in a number of neoclouds, including CoreWeave and Nebius, faces its own fresh turbulence after CEO Jensen Huang admitted Sunday that he'd had an extramarital affair with a business executive who intelligence agencies say is a Chinese spy. That news could add further volatility to an already nervous market.

Democrats last week won governors' races in Georgia, Iowa, Nevada, Ohio and Texas, and voters rejected data centers in all but one of the 41 related measures on ballots across the country.

CoreWeave CEO Mike Intrator took to LinkedIn to try to temper investors' fears.

"We currently don't anticipate any impact to CoreWeave's existing customer commitments or associated backlog as a result of shifts in the political landscape," Intrator wrote.

But the market was unrelenting. Yields on some CoreWeave bonds maturing in 2031 jumped above 16%. The spread on its five-year credit default swaps, which are insurance against default, soared above 1,240 basis points, up 50% in the past month.

"I do agree completely that neoclouds in particular are going to be the most impacted," Anastasia Amoroso, chief investment strategist at Partners Group, told CNBC's "Closing Bell" on Friday. Unlike hyperscalers Amazon and Microsoft, the top neoclouds are unprofitable and heavily dependent on a small number of customers.

IREN, a smaller publicly traded neocloud, said in its earnings report late Thursday that about 48% of its backlog was tied to Microsoft. But the company provided some relief to the market after reporting that AI cloud revenue more than doubled from the prior period. IREN's stock jumped 20% on Friday, recouping its losses from Thursday.

Daniel Roberts, co-CEO of IREN, told CNBC that there's a "disconnect between what we're seeing in public market and what we're seeing at the customer level," but he acknowledged the challenge posed by financial markets.

"Capital markets have become more selective in the last few weeks, and the questions have undoubtedly moved from how big the contract is to whether it can be delivered, financed, and run at a profit," Roberts said on the earnings call. His company currently has $10 billion in debt.

At the end of June, CoreWeave was sitting on $5.5 billion in cash and $35 billion in debt. The company owed $1.3 billion at a 15% interest rate on a facility that comes due in March 2028, and had $3.2 billion in obligations on an August 2030 facility at an 11% rate, according to the latest earnings statement.

CoreWeave is unlikely to go bankrupt in the near future, and it has the funds to deliver on existing contracts, but growth over the next five years will require more capital, said Luke Yang, a Morningstar analyst covering CoreWeave.

"If they want to issue money through equities, the stock price is not working in their favor, because the stock has been beaten down," Yang said. "So I also want to see how management talks about how to close this financing gap."

Yang said he's also paying attention to the broader neocloud market.

Nscale, based in the U.K., filed paperwork for an IPO in September but hasn't published an updated prospectus since then. "I wouldn't say it's a good time for them to go public," Yang said.

SB Energy, which filed at the beginning of September, is in a similar predicament, and Australian neocloud Firmus nixed plans for an IPO last month.

The uncertainty has not helped CoreWeave's case, Intrator told employees in an October memo seen by CNBC."But these companies are trying to raise enormous amounts of capital without the execution, revenue, scale or software solutions that CoreWeave delivers to its customers," he wrote.Investors are still on watch. Paul Meeks, head of technology research at Freedom Capital Markets, told CNBC's "Power Lunch" on Thursday that the narrative is "just so negative."

"Unfortunately, it's a self-fulfilling prophecy, a doom cycle, where if the creditors are worried about CoreWeave paying back their debts, then of course they have to borrow going forward to fund their AI infrastructure build at exorbitant rates," he said.

CoreWeave generated $5 billion in revenue in 2025. Meeks estimates that number jumping to $42 billion in 2028, with an EBITDA (earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization) margin that year of close to 70%.

"If they can get on that path and folks feel comfortable with that path, then they'll feel more comfortable about the debts and CoreWeave's financial wherewithal to pay those debts."

For the most recent quarter, analysts expect revenue growth of about 160% from a year earlier to $3.55 billion, according to LSEG. Nebius is expected to report sixfold growth to about $908 million.

Meeks said he expects the market to get "some relief" from both earnings reports.

CoreWeave declined to comment. Nebius didn't respond to a request for comment.

For Meeks, the key is getting capital markets back on board with the neoclouds' growth story, and believing that profits are on the way.

"You can have a healthy bank, but if there's a run on banks generally speaking then they could be in some trouble," he said. "So I am not fool enough to downplay the credit risk."

#115. Doing these things now will help you save money at Christmas

How soon is too soon when it comes to thinking about Christmas and your finances?

The answer, of course, is that it is never too soon. Save early and the cost becomes more manageable.

The squeeze is harder for some than others, with charity Barnardo's warning that some parents will skip meals so their kids get a Christmas dinner with the trimmings.

But, debt advice charity StepChange is offering tips to everyone, to avoid Christmas spending leading to a financially painful new year.

The best way to avoid a difficult January, it says, is to make a realistic plan before the spending begins.

Small decisions now can make a big difference later. Its tips include:

Budget for Christmas in full. So, that's not just presents, a budget should include the cost of food and drink, travel, parties, presents, postage, decorations and other festive extras

Spread the cost. Don't just rely on your December paypacket. Also, make a shopping list before Black Friday on 27 November - it is only a discount if you were going to buy it anyway

Book your train journey now. Advance tickets, if available, are cheaper

Set spending limits. Put a cap on the bill for Christmas socialising, and have a quiet conversation with family and friends about how much to spend on presents

Stocktake before the big food shop. Check your cupboards, fridge and freezer before you go shopping and plan meals around food you already have. Make a list of what you need and compare prices.

Think twice. Can you afford what you are going to buy? Can you afford the repayments if it's bought on credit? And, do you really need it?

"Christmas is a time when we want to enjoy ourselves and make things special for the people we care most about," said Vikki Brownridge, chief executive of StepChange.

"But it can also put a lot of pressure on household budgets, and it is key to take steps to get ahead of any January financial blues."

If you are concerned about Christmas leaving you in debt, then it is best to seek advice well before things become unmanageable.

The independent MoneyHelper website has a guide to finding free debt advice near you, external.

There are also tools to work out if you are receiving all the benefits and grants you are entitled to, and tips on how to draw up a budget.

And it is really important to watch out for scams at this time of year. Fraudsters will take any opportunity to catch you out, especially when you are under pressure financially.

#114. Cosm rides premium moviegoing wave with more immersive screenings and a wider library

The next big thing in premium large format theatrical viewing takes inspiration from the stars.

Cosm, an immersive entertainment technology company, got its start programming planetariums — its name a combination of "cosmos" and "colosseum." The company's massive, domed screens first offered guests a view of the universe, now they mimic courtside seats at sports venues around the world and drop film fans into some of Hollywood's most iconic sets.

At a time when the theatrical industry is seeing its biggest growth from the sale of premium large format, or PLF, tickets, Cosm seeks to offer a fresh take on an elevated viewing experience. And it's quickly expanding both its footprint and its content library.

Since 2024, Cosm has opened four theaters — near Los Angeles, Dallas, Atlanta and Detroit — with a Cleveland hub set to open in 2027. These experimental venues have garnered the attention of sports fans, screening live matchups in an arena-like environment. Showings often sell out, according to Cosm.

More recently, Cosm has partnered with Hollywood studios like Warner Bros., now a part of Skydance, to bring the likes of "The Matrix," "Willy Wonka & the Chocolate Factory," "Harry Potter and the Sorcerer's Stone" and "It" to its locations. Cosm recently signed a deal with Universal, which will bring "Shrek" to its domes in December.

The agreements mark a lucrative new chapter for the privately held Cosm, expanding its audience reach and viewing opportunities beyond timed, live events.

As a privately held company, Cosm doesn't disclose its financials. Tickets cost anywhere from $18 for a matinee to around $70 for prime seats or in-demand showings. That doesn't include added fees for concessions or items from special, themed menus tied to screenings.

Sony invested $100 million in the company in June, taking a minority stake and placing Sony Pictures Entertainment Chairman and CEO Ravi Ahuja onto its board of directors.

Cosm also counts among its investors Cosm chairman Steve Winn's firm, Mirasol Capital; former Milwaukee Bucks co-owner Marc Lasry's Avenue Sports Fund; Rocket Mortgage co-founder Dan Gilbert's Rock; and David Blitzer, managing partner of the New Jersey Devils and Philadelphia 76ers, who invested through his firm Bolt Ventures.

"Cosm is really early in its growth trajectory at the moment, and we saw an opportunity to partner with the company at, what I would say is at, a very important stage in its development," Jay Levine, chief strategy and business operations officer at Sony, told CNBC. "They have about five locations with the goal of a pretty significant rollout. And the real reason why we did it was it gave us a lot of exposure to a growing distribution and what we deem an experiential platform that we think has long-term potential."

Sony has made several plays in the theatrical space in recent years. It bought Alamo Drafthouse in 2024 and entered a long-term lease to reopen and restore the iconic Cinerama Dome in Los Angeles in July of this year.

"Our experiences strategy is around tech, it's around fandom, and it's around new and innovative approaches to content distribution," Levine said. "For us, we're all about these differentiated experiences."

Cosm's 87-foot-diameter, concave screens drop viewers into the middle of the action, whether it's an NFL game or inside the world of "The Matrix." The company's forthcoming Cleveland location will have an even bigger dome, at 100 feet in diameter.

The experience is akin to that of the Sphere in Las Vegas, which has garnered headlines since opening in 2023 for hosting high-profile concerts and events.

"People absolutely love to live out what they love in fandom, just like wearing a sports jersey," Alexis Scalice, head of Cosm Studios, told CNBC. "And I love that I'm seeing that same behavior happen with cinema."

Stadium-style seating is nestled within each Cosm dome, with booths and tables set on each level. The dome's width is such that it fills much of the viewer's periphery, giving the sensation that they are part of the on-screen environment.

The Los Angeles and Dallas locations have capacity for around 350 seats each, while the newer domes in Atlanta and Detroit have up to 500 available seats. Cosm has not shared how many seats the Cleveland location will have, but with its larger screen it is expected to have a larger number of seats.

For live sports events, Cosm has specialty cameras mounted in arenas that broadcast back to the dome, creating the illusion of being on the field of play.

For theatrical features, the film is played in a large rectangle in the center of the dome and the background is transformed to extend the scene.

In Cosm's presentation of "Harry Potter and the Sorcerer's Stone," for example, during the iconic scene where thousands of Hogwarts acceptance letters flood through the fireplace and into a living room, the background is swarmed with animated envelopes.

Cosm has partnered with experiential production company Little Cinema and creative studio MakeMake to create the visuals and environments for each scene of a film released in the dome. From storyboarding to post-production, this process can take up to a year as Cosm, the participating studio and the visual effects artists craft and render each moment.

"We really work hard not to take away from the story, so the film remains the hero in the narrative," Scalice said. "Working very closely with Warner Bros. and our creative partners to make sure that we're not detracting too much from the story, but we're enhancing those moments for more elevated emotional response."

With the exception of "It," which was released in 2017, Cosm's theatrical offerings thus far have been films that were released decades ago and that typically have long-established fan bases.

That helps Cosm leverage what Scalice called "that nostalgia factor" — "parents coming in that ... grew up on this, and they want to share it with their children."

"We can reinvigorate these legendary films," she added.

Cosm has dabbled across genres — horror, fantasy, science fiction — and also demographics — family-friendly and R-rated. "Shrek," set to debut Dec. 16, will be its first foray into animation.

"The thing that's so incredible about it is everybody thought initially it's just going to be working for sports, and when you look at what they've done, a lot of the titles with 'Harry Potter,' with 'Shrek,' and some of the other ones, I mean, the fill rates are actually incredible," Sony's Levine said.

Studios and theatrical exhibitors alike are looking for innovative ways to showcase Hollywood's product to moviegoers, especially as major shifts in viewing habits have disrupted the business.

In the post-pandemic era, the cinema space has been defined by fewer screens and fewer moviegoers, but those declines have been masked by higher movie ticket prices, particularly for PLF experiences.

These elevated offerings — think Imax, Dolby Cinema, ScreenX and 4DX — have bigger screens and better sound or introduce a unique experience, such as moving seats and fog machines. They're particularly popular with Gen Z and millennials, who've yet to be deterred by rising average ticket prices.

While an average standard movie ticket costs around $13, premium tickets average more than $18 each, according to data from EntTelligence.

Prices for Cosm's events fluctuate based on showtime and venue, but on average it's "higher than I think anybody really would have thought," Sony's Levine said.

Unlike Cosm's sports offerings, which can also be viewed from outside the dome from its hall and deck viewing areas at a lower price, theatrical presentations are only available in the dome.

"They have no problem selling these tickets because people want to experience these things, like an immersive format," said Mike Polydoros, CEO at cinema marketing firm PaperAirplane Media. "That is something they can't get at home."

#113. Many workers lose unused FSA money — but could still come out ahead. Here's how

Health flexible spending accounts, or FSAs, are tax-advantaged accounts that let workers set aside pretax money to cover eligible health expenses. However, they're often use it or lose it, with unspent balances forfeited at the end of the plan year.

About 4 in 10 workers with health FSAs forfeited unused money in 2024, according to an October report from the Employee Benefit Research Institute, a nonprofit research organization. Among those FSA account holders, the average forfeiture was $404, down from $436 in 2023. The research examined more than 3.3 million accounts representing $3.9 billion in contributions.

Yet even workers who don't zero out their balance may still benefit from using an FSA, compared with what they would have spent using after-tax dollars to cover the same expenses.

"Even if someone forfeits FSA funds back to their employer, they can still come out ahead — relative to not participating in an FSA — if the amount they forfeited is less than the tax savings they realized," said Jake Spiegel, a senior research associate for EBRI and author of the study.

Nearly three-quarters — 72% — of state and local government workers and 47% of private-sector workers have access to a health FSA in 2026, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics. That compares with 68% and 40%, respectively, in 2017.

FSAs differ from health savings accounts, or HSAs, which some people use in conjunction with high-deductible health plans. Although both let you set aside pretax money for health care expenses, HSAs let you roll over your balance from year to year, and even invest it.

Roughly a third of FSA users in the EBRI database face a strict use-it-or-lose-it provision, Spiegel said. The rest either are permitted by their employers to carry over an amount — up to $680 in 2026 — into the following year or are offered a grace period of up to 2.5 months after the deadline to incur additional expenses or face forfeiture. However, employers cannot offer both options.

While many plans have a Dec. 31 deadline for spending FSA money, some employers have deadlines falling in other parts of the year, said Rachel Rouleau, chief compliance officer for Health-E Commerce, parent company of online retailers FSA Store and HSA Store.

"Understanding these plan-specific deadlines is essential, so employees should check with their employer or FSA administrator to avoid forfeiting unused funds," Rouleau said.

Additionally, many employers give you extra time — typically three months after the deadline — to submit receipts for qualifying expenses that occurred during the previous plan year.

For 2026, the maximum contribution allowed to health FSAs is $3,400. In 2027, that amount will be $3,500, the IRS announced Thursday.

Part of the reason workers have forfeitures may be that they must decide how much they want to contribute to their FSA before the plan year begins.

"It's difficult to project how much you'll need to spend on health care, especially if you are particularly healthy and don't have a chronic condition with predictable prescription drug costs," Spiegel said.

Contributions to FSAs are withheld from paychecks and generally avoid federal taxes, as well as so-called payroll taxes — known as FICA — that go toward Social Security and Medicare. They also may avoid state and local taxes, depending on where you live.

To determine how much you're saving in taxes by using an FSA, you can add up those tax rates and apply the total to your contribution, said certified financial planner Sean Lovison, founder of Purpose Built Financial Services in Moorestown, New Jersey.

For example, say that your federal, FICA, state and local taxes total 35% and you contributed the maximum allowed — $3,400 for 2026. In this case, the calculation would result in $1,190 in estimated tax savings, which is "how much you're saving on taxes by contributing to your FSA," Lovison said.

The illustration assumes the worker receives no employer contribution to the FSA and the tax rate applies to the entire contribution.

"As long as the number you're forfeiting from your FSA is less than that number, you're going to come out ahead," Lovison said.

Of course, he said, "you don't want to walk away from FSA money, but at the same time, from a mathematical standpoint, you're still coming out ahead."

If you face a forfeiture, you can use it to help inform your decision on how much to put toward your FSA for 2027, if you haven't already made benefit elections for next year.

"Employees should look at their anticipated healthcare expenses over the coming year and consider both their daily health routines and expected annual expenses," Rouleau said. "This includes recurring prescriptions, doctor visits, copays, deductibles, dental and vision care, and other eligible out-of-pocket healthcare expenses for themselves and their eligible dependents."

Before you resign yourself to a forfeiture, keep in mind that there are also many ways to use the money on eligible expenses before the end of the year.

"There are thousands of ways consumers can responsibly spend down their FSA dollars, from everyday healthcare essentials to eligible telehealth services," Rouleau said.

Additionally, "many consumers are surprised to learn that a wide range of over-the-counter medications, menstrual care products, sunscreen, first-aid supplies and certain health-monitoring devices are FSA-eligible," Rouleau said.

That's in addition to doctor and dentist appointments, prescription drugs and other healthcare services such as acupuncture and addiction treatment, according to the IRS.

If you're unsure whether you have money remaining in your FSA or need to spend it by the end of the year, most plans offer an online portal where you can review your balance, submit claims and check deadlines. The FSA administrator's information is typically listed on the back of your FSA card, Rouleau said.

#110. Using miles to fly for Thanksgiving? These are the most expensive and cheapest routes

Airline ticket prices have surged this year, especially for travel over the holidays. Don't expect many bargains if you're booking flights with miles, either.

Tickets bought with frequent flyer miles for flights between large U.S. airports over the Thanksgiving travel period will cost 32% more than last year's holiday, according to data from Seats.aero, a miles and points redemption search site. While fares have skyrocketed this year, airline executives last month said demand remains strong.

Airlines have largely gotten rid of static mileage redemption charts in favor of dynamic pricing, making budgeting for an award ticket more difficult. Dynamic pricing means redemption costs change depending on demand, routes and other factors.

Many of the most expensive routes this Thanksgiving are understandably longer, including from Hawaii, but the list also includes a nonstop from New York to Houston.

Here's how mileage redemptions for the holiday are stacking up, based on one-way flights on average as of early November and compared with last year:

Jet fuel prices have surged since the U.S. and Israel attacked Iran on Feb. 28, which has eaten into airline profits. Carriers have sought to pass costs along to consumers as much as possible.

Even some of the cheapest routes for award tickets are more expensive than last year, including for short routes like Tulsa, Oklahoma, to Dallas Fort Worth International Airport.

#109. These states are offering extra ACA premium subsidies in 2027

Health care coverage in some states is going to get slightly more affordable in 2027.

At least 11 states are providing funding to Obamacare enrollees to reduce their monthly insurance premiums, which qualifying enrollees should see when they sign up for coverage this open enrollment season.

The move follows a lapse of enhanced federal premium subsidies in 2025 that drove up costs for millions of people who buy health plans on the Affordable Care Act marketplace. The states include California, Colorado, Connecticut, Maryland, Massachusetts, New Mexico, New Jersey, Rhode Island, Vermont, Virginia and Washington state.

Most of the states are continuing policies they put in place for 2026, in the aftermath of the loss of the federal subsidies. But Rhode Island and Virginia are debuting their respective programs for 2027, while other states like California expanded eligibility to additional consumers.

Health policy experts said the state funding could help prevent more consumers from dropping their insurance coverage, following a significant drop decline this year. It also comes amid a broader affordability crunch for U.S. households, which voters cited as a top issue in the recent midterm elections.

While enhanced federal premium subsidies lapsed at the end of 2025, there's still a tranche of federal subsidies — known as premium tax credits — that remain in place. The funding offered by these 11 states comes on top of any federal subsidies consumers may receive.

The amount of financial assistance varies by state, and each state has different eligibility criteria. Many target their programs at lower-income consumers, experts said.

"This was an effort to make coverage more affordable again" for some people, said Louise Norris, a health policy analyst for insurance referral site healthinsurance.org. "It can certainly be significant if you qualify for the help your state provides."

The states offering additional premium assistance operate state-based health insurance marketplaces rather than HealthCare.gov. They're also largely Democratic-leaning states, health policy experts said.

Most offered premium assistance to state residents in 2026. However, Rhode Island and Virginia are new to the list for 2027.

In many of the states, officials said the move to offer state-funded premium assistance is a direct response to the lapse of enhanced ACA premium subsidies at the end of 2025.

After the lapse of the enhanced subsidies — which had been in effect since 2021 and helped push ACA enrollment to record highs — Obamacare premiums increased by an average of 58%, to $178 per month on average in 2026 from $113 per month the prior year, according to KFF, a nonpartisan health policy research group.

Democrats pushed to renew the enhanced premium subsidies — the crux of a government shutdown in 2025 that lasted more than a month — but the Republican-led Congress opted not to extend them.

ACA enrollment fell to 19.2 million as of February 2026, a 13% decline from 22.1 million in February 2025, according to figures published in June by the U.S. Department of Health and Human Services.

Evidence suggests enrollment has fallen further since then, according to a KFF analysis.

Health policy experts largely attribute the decline to the expiration of enhanced subsidies, since the lapse pushed up costs for consumers.

"Where Congress has failed, Colorado is stepping up," Gov. Jared Polis said in September in a press release discussing insurance premium assistance. "Despite the loss of the enhanced premium tax credits, we are doing everything we can to keep health insurance prices down so Coloradans can afford access to health care."

Not all consumers enrolled in an ACA marketplace health plan will be eligible for their state's financial assistance.

Of all the states, New Mexico is the only one that is fully replacing the enhanced federal premium subsidies for ACA enrollees in the state in 2027, extending a policy in place for 2026, according to health policy experts.

While ACA enrollment declined nationally in 2026, New Mexico was the only state to see an increase in its enrollment figures, growing 14% between 2025 and 2026, according to KFF.

Some states allocated more funding to their premium assistance programs for 2027 relative to 2026.

In California, for example, Gov. Gavin Newsom, a Democrat, and the California Legislature increased the amount of state funds available for the Covered California State Subsidy Program to $300 million in 2027 from $190 million in 2026, according to a press release on the state's health insurance marketplace.

Californians with incomes up to 200% of the federal poverty level in 2027 — $31,920 for an individual or $66,000 for a family of four — will be eligible for a state premium subsidy. In 2026, those subsidies are available for households with incomes up to 165% of the federal poverty line.

As a result of the eligibility expansion, an additional 200,000 will be eligible for financial help who weren't eligible in 2026, Covered California, the state marketplace, said in a press release.

Massachusetts also increased its investment in financial assistance for ACA enrollees to $620 million in 2027 from $600 million this year — the largest state investment in the country, state officials said in October.

The value of state assistance can be modest for some consumers, depending on factors like their income and monthly premiums, health policy experts said.

For example, in Washington state, ACA enrollees with an income up to 200% of the federal poverty line and who receive a federal subsidy can get a state-funded subsidy of up to $35 per month. Those with an income between 201% and 250% of the poverty line can get up to $20 per month.

That said, states that offer premium subsidies saw a lower decline in ACA marketplace enrollment than in states that didn't offer any financial assistance, experts said.

"We saw that states in 2026 that did offer assistance had smaller declines in enrollment," said Justin Lo, a senior researcher at KFF who specializes in the Affordable Care Act. "If the previous pattern continues, we will also see possibly better enrollment retention in states that are more generous with their subsidies."

It's not a guarantee states will be able to continue funding these subsidies into the future, experts said.

"Funding is obviously the crux of the issue," said Norris, of healthinsurance.org. "Whether states will be able to continue to provide funding for these programs really depends on these states' budgets."

#107. What we know so far about Jensen Huang's affair with suspected Chinese spy

Jensen Huang admitted having an extramarital affair and said he would cooperate with an internal security investigation

One of the Artificial Intelligence (AI) industry's most important figures has been accused of having an affair with a suspected Chinese spy, prompting an investigation.

US intelligence officials believed that Chinese businesswoman Alice Huang, who had a close relationship with Nvidia CEO and co-founder Jensen Huang, was a spy for Beijing, according to a New York Times report., external

On Sunday, Huang admitted, external to having been "unfaithful" to his wife and apologised for hurting her and their children.

US tech giant Nvidia, one of the most valuable companies in the world, has opened an independent investigation into the reports. The firm said it does not believe US national security had been compromised - and added that Huang was committed to continuing to lead the company.

Here is what we know about the affair and why it matters.

Why Trump is all-in on AI despite the warnings

What is AI, how does it work and why are some people concerned about it?

Jensen Huang (left) and Alice Huang - also known as Huang Le - pictured at an event

US officials believed that Alice Huang had been recruited to spy by China's top intelligence agency, the New York Times reported. Her current whereabouts are unknown.

Alice Huang, who is of no relation to Jensen Huang, was a top executive at Singapore-based data centre firm Megaspeed between March and November 2023. The firm had been suspected of helping divert Nvidia chips to China, a concern that Nvidia maintains was investigated and debunked.

However, the BBC understands Megaspeed is under investigation by the Singapore government regarding previous allegations that the company was circumventing US sanctions on the export of Nvidia's high-end chips.

The company told the New York Times last month its current leadership took the allegations seriously and had appointed external legal advisers to conduct an internal investigation.

Surveillance reportedly revealed communications between Alice Huang and an American citizen, who was subsequently identified as Jensen Huang through a process known as "unmasking".

It remains unclear what, if any, intelligence Alice Huang â who is also known as Huang Le â allegedly gathered and passed to Beijing. She did not respond to the paper's request for comment.

When the BBC asked Nvidia if the company was aware of intelligence concerns, the company said it was not.

"We trust that if we were the target of a genuine threat, the US government would have notified us," an Nvidia spokesperson told the BBC.

China has rejected the spying allegations.

Foreign Ministry spokesperson Guo Jiakun told reporters on Monday at a regular daily press briefing: "We have already responded to this. The allegations about Chinese spies are entirely groundless, speculative and intended to smear and malign China."

The New York Times reported in late October that Trump had been briefed last year on intelligence alleging Alice Huang was spying on China's behalf.

Asked by reporters on Sunday whether he wished he had "acted on the intelligence", Trump responded: "No, I don't think so. I think he's an honourable guy."

US Secretary of State Marco Rubio, meanwhile, refused to comment when asked about Huang. Last spring, the secretary of state reportedly oversaw the drafting of a memo that warned the Trump administration about Huang's relationship with the Chinese businesswoman, according to the New York Times.

Nvidia is at the heart of US and China's AI race, which is why the billionaire's affair has sparked national security fears among American lawmakers.

Trump has said "whoever wins AI wins", and the fear is that if China gains the upper hand in AI, it will supercharge the country's military and surveillance capabilities.

The US advantage is in large part because of US companies like Nvidia, working with producers like the Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Company (TSMC), which makes more than 90% of the world's most advanced chips.

Since 2022, the US has used various export controls to keep the top tech out of China, which it says helps retain America's competitive edge and protects its national security.

This means companies like Nvidia have been largely locked out of the world's most lucrative market, with Huang once saying that Nvidia's share of the Chinese market had gone from around 95% to effectively zero.

Huang has engaged in shuttle diplomacy between Washington and Beijing, hoping to find a US-approved way back in, and shaping US policy on China along the way.

Sunday's revelations may explain why Huang has failed to bring home the biggest prize of all to Nvidia: the Trump administration's blessing to sell all but its most advanced chips to China.

Huang has soared to international importance with his company

Born in Taiwan in 1963, Huang was nine years old when his family moved to the West Coast of the US. He graduated as an electrical engineer from Oregon State University, and then with a master's degree in the same field from Stanford University.

Huang then worked at US chip firm AMD before co-founding Nvidia. Known as "Daddy Huang" in Taiwan and once lauded as the "Taylor Swift of tech", he has a reputation as a workaholic and perfectionist with incredibly high standards for his staff.

He has an estimated fortune of $155bn (Â£115bn), according to the Bloomberg Billionaires Index.

"Jensen Huang is a tech visionary of the likes of Steve Jobs," Stephen Witt, the author of Thinking Machine, a biography on Huang, told the BBC, adding that he was responsible for creating AI hardware as we know it today.

Huang has a famously close relationship with Trump. At the recent AI summit at the White House, where 42 tech executives were present, the president was flanked by Elon Musk and Huang.

Trump said he had not spoken to the Nvidia boss since news of the investigation emerged.

"I just know he's a fine person, but you never know what's going on," Trump told reporters, adding that he had "done an amazing job for our country".

There is no clear successor for Huang at Nvidia

Founded by Huang and fellow electrical engineers Chris Malachowsky and Curtis Priem in 1993, the company was originally known for chips that process graphics, particularly for computer games.

Eventually it changed focus to AI, a field it now dominates.

Huang rescued Nvidia from bankruptcy on more than one occasion, and took risks on AI a long time before the rest of the world did, effectively building the infrastructure from scratch.

"Today when we think of that, we think of these giant data centres - none of that existed. There were no customers for the stuff he was building. In fact his first customer was Open AI at the time they had 30 people - he actually gave it to them, he donated it to them," Witt told the BBC.

Every notable tech company building AI tools and infrastructure, including Amazon, Meta, Google, and Microsoft, now use Nvidia chips.

Currently worth $4.5tn (Â£3.4tn), Nvidia is one of the most valuable companies ever.

For now, the firm's board appears to have little appetite for removing Huang, saying in a statement on Sunday that they have "confidence in Jensen and his leadership team".

Sources tell the BBC his removal is unlikely because there is so much money at stake.

There is no public succession plan for the 63-year-old. He has often said he hopes to continue in the role for decades, and Bloomberg reports he has created an informal leadership structure around him unlike a traditional company's hierarchy.

His children, Spencer and Madison, and son-in-law Nico Caprez work at Nvidia in senior roles.

Lori Huang accompanied her husband at the state dinner held by President Donald Trump for his Chinese counterpart Xi Jinping in September

Huang met his future wife Lori Mills at Oregon State University while studying electrical engineering in the early 1980s. The pair were lab partners.

They married in 1985, about eight years before he co-founded Nvidia, and have two children, Madison and Spencer.

In 2007, the couple founded the Jen-Hsun and Lori Huang Foundation. The charitable fund has seen its assets surge in recent years, driven by the soaring value of Nvidia shares, and is estimated to be worth more than $9bn.

Its donations include tens of millions of dollars to engineering programmes at Oregon State and Stanford University.

Witt told the BBC that Huang has gone to great lengths to present himself as a family man, adding: "That's very important to him".

"He praises his wife, he talks about her in very glowing ways," he added. "The fallout of this would be bad for anyone's family. But for someone who has gone to such great lengths to cultivate an image of being a family guy and who keeps his children very close, it is very bad."

Additional reporting by Tom McArthur in London, Ayeshea Perera and Tessa Wong in Singapore and Helen Davidson in Sydney

#106. Civil servant who juggled five jobs found guilty of fraud

Civil servant Kashim Chowdhury juggled multiple public sector jobs

A civil servant who secretly held multiple public sector jobs on full-time salaries has been found guilty of fraud.

Kashim Chowdhury, from east London, had five government jobs that overlapped in the spring of 2023. He fraudulently earned more than Â£284,000 of taxpayers' money.

The prosecution accused the 56-year-old of ''deliberate, intentional dishonesty'' and of ''exploiting the system''.

A jury at Southwark Crown Court found him guilty of nine counts of fraud, including making false representations and of making a dishonest gain.

#104. Poppi founders are going after PepsiCo's Gatorade with new beverage startup Jetty

After disrupting the soda category with Poppi, founders Allison and Stephen Ellsworth are now taking aim at hydration with their new brand, Jetty.

Starting Monday, Jetty drinks and ready-to-mix electrolyte packets will be on shelves at Whole Foods grocery stores and available online at Target and Amazon.

The new, self-funded venture directly pits the Ellsworths against PepsiCo, which bought Poppi for $1.95 billion in 2025. Pepsi owns Gatorade, which dominates the U.S. sports drink category with about 60% market share, according to Numerator data.

Poppi shook up the soda category with prebiotic fiber, low calorie and sugar counts and its fun marketing aimed at Generation Z and millennials. Jetty looks to upend the hydration category through a similar strategy. To do so, it will face off not only against Gatorade but also a swarm of other upstarts looking to get in on the electrolyte-fueled trend.

For decades, Gatorade and other sports drinks have pitched their products to athletes who need electrolytes and fluids after an intense workout, although many consumers drink them every day without even sweating. Jetty is aimed at people who need a hydration boost, but not the same level of electrolytes or sugar as a traditional sports drink.

"No one has really created a product for just everyday hydration — literally everyday hydration for normal people," Allison Ellsworth, Jetty's chief brand officer, told CNBC. "Like I woke up this morning, I didn't run a marathon."

Following Poppi's sale to PepsiCo, Ellsworth stayed on as an advisor to the brand, although she said she is no longer involved in the day-to-day operations.

After 10 years building Poppi, Ellsworth said, she finally had free time. As Ellsworth tells it, she went to the doctor for the first time in a decade. She started using a GLP-1 medication and lost 60 pounds.

Ellsworth's husband, Stephen, now CEO of the new brand, noticed she wasn't hydrating, even when she came back from the gym.

GLP-1 medications such as Ozempic and Mounjaro can make people less thirsty. But because staying hydrated can also help mitigate some of the drugs' side effects, such as nausea and headaches, it can become more important.

"I physically struggle getting liquid into my body," Ellsworth said.

Plain old water is often less appealing to consumers, even dehydrated GLP-1 users, but more flavorful options with added benefits can be tempting. About 44% of GLP-1 users report consuming more functional beverages, according to EY-Parthenon's third annual survey of more than 3,600 current or former GLP-1 users.

Ellsworth sees a broader appeal for Jetty, too.

"Our core audience is going to be millennial moms and Gen Z," she said. "Gen Z just isn't relating to these old school brands, the legacy of the Gatorades and the Powerades. They're just not cool."

Consumers broadly have become more interested in staying hydrated.

Dollar sales of hydration and electrolyte products climbed 20% to $2.6 billion over the 52 weeks ended Oct. 4, based on data from SPINS, which tracks new universal product codes, or UPCs. The category also has a strong repeat buyer rate of 62%.

However, the Ellsworths aren't the only ones to notice the potential in hydration. The number of new items in the category with annual sales of at least $10,000 has increased 41% over the last two years, according to SPINS.

Big food and beverage companies have been looking to hydration, too.

Kraft Heinz debuted Capri Sun juice pouches and Kool-Aid packets with electrolytes this spring. In April, Pepsi unveiled an overhaul of the Gatorade brand, focused on making it more appealing to a wider swath of consumers, with more potential occasions like a "fresh morning start" or an "afternoon walk." And Electrolit is a top performer for its distributor Keurig Dr Pepper, with 40% retail sales growth in the company's third quarter.

All of that means more competition for shelf space and consumers' "share of throat," an industry term for beverage market share.

To increase Jetty's visibility across the grocery store and to maximize convenience for shoppers, Jetty will be available in both powders and bottles.

Single-use electrolyte mixes are growing at a much faster clip than the ready-to-drink category, Ellsworth said, thanks to Unilever's Liquid I.V. and smaller upstarts like LMNT. On Amazon alone, powders are a $1 billion category, according to Ellsworth.

The ready-to-drink, or RTD, sports beverages are a much larger market, but the category's sales are shrinking as consumers reach for other hydration options instead. Unit sales of sports drinks have fallen 9.1% from 2022 to 2026, according to Numerator data. And Coca-Cola has written down the value of BodyArmor by more than $1.7 billion after buying control of the brand for $5.6 billion in late 2021.

"Me and my husband know how to do RTD," Ellsworth said.

Jetty will launch with five flavors, including watermelon, orange mango, fruit punch, black raspberry and white cherry. All of them contain 100 milligrams of sodium, 310 milligrams of potassium and 100 milligrams of magnesium in a 15.2-ounce bottle or 15-gram packet.

In addition to electrolytes, Jetty products also contain B vitamins, 3 grams of fiber and 6 grams of sugar. It doesn't use artificial sweeteners or dyes, its founders say.

For comparison, a 20-ounce bottle of traditional Gatorade Thirst Quencher has about 270 milligrams of sodium and 80 milligrams of potassium, plus 34 grams of sugar. The sports drink brand is also phasing out artificial colors and promoting lower-sugar versions.

"What's really interesting about this product and this opportunity is we now can capture all those lapsed drinkers — or that generation that didn't even ever drink it in the first place," Ellsworth said.

Poppi's new owner knows about Jetty and the market it is trying to capture, too.

"I don't think [Pepsi] gets it, but what they do get is that the movement is changing, and they think that just by taking dyes out or doing 90% less sugar, they're going to capture this audience," Ellsworth said. "But that is very big corporate thinking."

She added that she believes Pepsi has missed the mark on advertising to Gen Z consumers across its portfolio, such as in its campaigns for Sprite competitor Starry.

Perhaps as an extension of that, Poppi's performance has been underwhelming under PepsiCo's ownership.

The food and beverage giant now estimates the fair value of potential performance-based payments to Poppi's prior owners is $12 million, Pepsi said in a regulatory filing in October. At the time of the deal, Pepsi promised an additional payment of $300 million if Poppi hit certain targets by the third quarter of 2027. The Ellsworths declined to comment on the filing to CNBC.

This time around, with one successful brand under their belts, Jetty's founders say they have learned from past mistakes. Those lessons should help Jetty as it launches at a much larger scale than Poppi did.

Three different claims lawyers have signed off on Jetty's packaging, according to Ellsworth. Poppi paid $8.9 million to settle class-action litigation alleging false advertising over its "gut healthy" claims.

For now, Jetty is entirely self-funded with $2.5 million, although Ellsworth said that the brand will likely raise money in 2027. This time, though, she said investors will probably look less like traditional venture capitalists.

"I want to bring in investors that bring value," Ellsworth said. Instead of a venture capitalist who is willing to invest $15 million, Jetty would rather consider investors who can only write a check for $1 million but have access to celebrities. "So then you're getting access to talent on a cheaper level and people that are invested in the brand."

And Jetty has also learned a few lessons from Gatorade. For example, the brand is already at work on a lemon-lime version — the original flavor of Gatorade.

"We'll eventually do it," Ellsworth said. "But it was really important at the beginning just to stick to flavors that we love, for it to taste good."

#103. Rubio sees little hope of diplomatic breakthrough to Mideast deadlock amid 'outrageous' Iranian demands

U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio has accused Iran of "outrageous and ridiculous" demands as efforts to reach a lasting resolution in the Middle East war appeared stalled.

Speaking with reporters in Florida on Sunday evening before traveling to Paris for the G7 foreign ministers' meeting, Rubio indicated the two sides remain far apart on negotiations, after Tehran proposed shortening the timetable for restarting talks from seven days to two.

Rubio said President Donald Trump would prefer to solve "this and every problem diplomatically," but added that "sometimes that's not possible, unfortunately."

Energy prices moved lower on Monday morning after President Trump said Sunday that further strikes were "highly unlikely" if negotiations progressed toward a strong agreement.

Brent crude, the international price benchmark, was down 0.75% at $97.58 per barrel by 5:25 a.m. ET. Meanwhile, U.S. West Texas Intermediate futures for December delivery were trading 0.42% lower at $90.45.

Trump said in a CNN interview prior to Rubio's comments that a resumption of hostilities in the more than eight-month-long conflict was unlikely, provided that talks to constrain Iran's nuclear ambitions and reopen the Strait of Hormuz can progress.

"I wouldn't say we're on the verge of a breakthrough," Rubio said. "Right now, it hasn't been very productive because the Iranian demands are simply not just unacceptable but, in some cases, outrageous and ridiculous and really not realistic."

Iranian Foreign Ministry spokesperson Esmaeil Baghaei said Monday that Tehran wants U.S. sanctions lifted and the war ended on all fronts before negotiations begin.

#101. Congress returns with government funding, Pete Hegseth and the debt limit on the agenda

The House and Senate are due to return to Washington on Monday with a crowded legislative agenda in the final weeks before the end of the 119th Congress.

Congress will need to act quickly to extend government funding, which expires Dec. 11. Lawmakers will need to pass the National Defense Authorization Act, and they may tackle ambitious proposals including a construction permitting package and a bill to regulate name, image and likeness rules in college sports.

The work will commence under a new dynamic. Republicans come back to the Capitol diminished after suffering substantial losses in this week's midterms. Democrats swept to a commanding majority in the House with at least 255 seats in the 435-member chamber and could emerge with as many as 56 seats in the Senate.

With majorities in both chambers looming, Democrats will have little incentive to cut deals with their GOP counterparts.

The most pressing concern lawmakers will face when they return is funding the government. Current funding expires Dec. 11. Congress must pass another continuing resolution to retain current spending levels or a full-year suite of appropriations bills before then to avoid a government shutdown.

Appropriations bills and continuing resolutions require bipartisan majorities due to the Senate's 60-vote filibuster threshold. Democrats are unlikely to negotiate a full year of government funding before the new Congress is sworn in, given that they will soon hold commanding majorities in both chambers and can use that to leverage concessions from the White House.

"This former preschool teacher is going to teach President Trump a simple lesson: The power of the purse belongs to CONGRESS!" Sen. Patty Murray, D-Wash., the presumptive chair of the Appropriations Committee, said in an X post Tuesday as election results rolled in. "It's NOT Trump's money. Taxpayer dollars belong to the American PEOPLE."

Adding another wrinkle to the funding push is the electoral loss of Sen. Susan Collins, R-Maine, who chairs the Senate Appropriations Committee and is one of the "four corners" of spending talks. Collins lost reelection to Democrat Troy Jackson, ending a five-term career in the Senate.

Some lawmakers, including Sen. Kevin Cramer, R-N.D., have called for Congress to raise the debt limit before Republicans lose control.

In September, the federal debt hit $40 trillion for the first time, bumping up against the $41.1 trillion ceiling written into the GOP's 2025 budget reconciliation law known as the "one big beautiful bill."

The Bipartisan Policy Center, a nonprofit that tracks the debt limit, said Treasury is likely to hit its maximum borrowing capacity by mid-summer. The U.S. may no longer be able to meet all its obligations by November 2027, according to a Bank of America estimate from Nov. 6.

But Democrats may instead prefer to use the debt limit as a bargaining chip in the next Congress.

Republicans could theoretically raise the debt ceiling in a budget reconciliation package, as they did with their 2025 law. But cobbling together a post-election coalition to clear another reconciliation bill with exceptionally narrow margins would be difficult. Budget reconciliation, which allows party-line bills to skirt the filibuster in the Senate, requires near-total unity.

Congressional leadership, particularly on the Republican side, is likely to have difficulty with lawmaker attendance after dozens of members lost their seats in an electoral bloodbath. Leadership will also struggle to whip members into line, especially those who do not have another election to run.

Top senators on energy committees struck a deal on a permitting bill before the election. That accord to make construction easier could now be in jeopardy, though the Senate plans to hold a procedural vote to advance it Monday evening, according to a Senate aide familiar with the schedule. The person asked not to be named because the move has not been announced.

The resounding majorities Democrats won in both the House and Senate may give them incentive to torpedo the permitting deal in hopes of securing a better one later. The deal also may have been in trouble prior to the election, with top Democrats in the House not supporting the version their counterparts in the Senate accepted.

Politico reported Thursday that Democratic Minority Leader and likely next House Speaker Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., did not support the permitting deal.

Lawmakers have long sought, and failed to reach, a bipartisan, bicameral deal on permitting reform. Democrats want to speed up the buildout of clean energy. Republicans have long complained that the federal permitting process and environmental laws that comprise it stymie the U.S.'s ability to build critical infrastructure.

The House passed a version of the NDAA, which would authorize more than $1 trillion in defense spending, in July. But Senate Democrats blocked their chamber's version of the bill that same month over concerns about the Iran war.

"Donald Trump is dragging America deeper into a war in Iran with no authorization, no plan, and no exit strategy. Democrats will not go along," Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer, D-N.Y., said in a statement at the time.

The NDAA is typically seen as a must-pass, bipartisan bill.

The legislation as it stood in July also drew pushback from businesses over a provision that would restrict Pentagon contractors from executing stock buybacks and paying dividends unless they have the Defense Department's approval.

A new wrinkle threatens to derail the NDAA process during the lame duck, however. Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth faces a vote on articles of impeachment upon Congress' return, and Democrats have already been highly skeptical of the Pentagon's asks in this year's budget as the expensive Iran war drags on.

Other priorities could also get floor time.

The Senate in September approved, in a bipartisan vote, a package that would establish federal rules for the ways in which college athletes are compensated. But that legislation has many opponents in the House, particularly among Democrats in the chamber, making its passage more complicated.

There's also been a yearslong effort to ban members of Congress from owning or trading individual stocks.

In previous Congresses, the effort was stymied by leadership from both parties. And more recently, a bipartisan push to pass legislation was derailed after House Democrats demanded the ban also apply to the president.

One GOP-led proposal, called the Stop Insider Trading Act, passed the House with some bipartisan support in July, but met with Democratic opposition because it would apply only to Congress and would not require lawmakers to divest existing holdings.

Sen. Todd Young, R-Ind., called for action on the issue in an X post Thursday. Young is a co-sponsor of the Senate version of the Stop Insider Trading Act.

"We have a great opportunity in the lame duck session to restore a small amount of trust by banning Members of Congress from trading stocks. I've been talking about this for many months, and it should be a no-brainer," Young said.

Democrats, however, are eager to claim a victory on congressional stock trading in the next Congress.

Jeffries, in a letter to fellow House members announcing his candidacy for speaker, listed it as a priority as part of Democrats' anti-corruption agenda.

"We must root out corrupt price gouging, clean up the mess at the Supreme Court and ban Members of Congress from trading stocks. Accountability begins at home," Jeffries wrote.

#100. Restaurant chains refocus on food quality to bring back diners

Juicier chicken tenders. Revamped sauces. Richer and creamier Frosties.

Across the restaurant industry, chains are turning their focus back to their food in the hopes that better quality will win back diners.

Over the last two years, consumers have dined out less frequently and grown pickier about where they choose to eat, based on data from Black Box Intelligence. In that time, "value" has been the buzzword du jour for restaurant executives, who have explained to analysts and investors that the word encompasses more than menu pricing alone.

Now, industry CEOs are turning to their corporate chefs to try to beat rivals on quality and taste, as they look to expand their definition of value. Discounts and one-off promotions can lift traffic for a time, but the actual product still matters for restaurants' long-term growth, as well as franchisee profitability.

From Wendy's to Chipotle Mexican Grill, chief executives touted new or upcoming improvements to menu items on their quarterly earnings conference calls in recent weeks.

"We think the product's improved and even better," Chipotle CEO Scott Boatwright told analysts on the company's call in late October, referring to the burrito chain's brisket, which returned to menus for a limited time Thursday.

For some chains, better food is a key step in ambitious turnaround plans.

For example, "quality food at real value" is one pillar of Wendy's five-part turnaround strategy that the chain unveiled on Thursday.

"We're also rebuilding our food offerings and have begun testing improvements to quality and craveability of our core items, including thicker, crispier bacon, a richer and creamier Frosty, whole leaf lettuce on sandwiches and improved salads," Wendy's CEO Bob Wright told analysts on the call Thursday.

That same day, the company reported its seventh straight quarter of same-store sales declines and publicly rejected a $1.4 billion takeover bid led by Nelson Peltz's Trian Partners. Wendy's said the proposal did not take into account the potential value that its turnaround plan could unlock.

There is precedent to suggest that better-tasting food can contribute to higher sales.

In 2009, Domino's Pizza changed its recipe, swapping out its sauce, adding garlic to its crust and switching to shredded mozzarella with a hint of provolone. Along with blunt commercials that compared its previous crust to cardboard and old sauce to ketchup, the changes helped to spur U.S. same-store sales growth of 9.9% the following year.

Now, some of the same team that led the Domino's turnaround is copying the playbook at Burger King's U.S. restaurants.

Part of the burger chain's multiyear comeback plan has focused on revamping its core menu items. It started earlier this year with its flagship offering, the Whopper, which received a better bun and a new mayonnaise. And in September, it rolled out an overhaul of its nuggets, switching to 100% white meat, crispier breading and a more flavorful marinade. It also reformulated its sauces.

"We've tried to look at our biggest categories first," Josh Kobza, CEO of Burger King parent Restaurant Brands International, told CNBC. "We've also thought about the ones where the fixes are more straightforward and less operationally involved."

Following the changes, Whopper sales were up by a double-digit percentage in the third quarter, Kobza said on the company's earnings call in late October. And nugget sales climbed more than 35%. Burger King said its U.S. same-store sales jumped 8.4% in the quarter.

Like Domino's, Burger King is highlighting diners' past gripes with its Whoppers and nuggets in its ads, which star Burger King U.S. president and former Domino's executive Tom Curtis.

Better-tasting food isn't just for chains that are in trouble.

Even Chili's, which has seen its same-store sales soar 52% over the past three years, is improving more of its existing menu options. After tackling its baby back ribs, burgers and nachos, it will turn next to salads and steaks, Brinker International CEO Kevin Hochman told CNBC.

"We're going to be doing more dry aging of our beef to make it more tender, versus doing it in a plant," he said. "It's a little bit more expensive, but we think the taste is going to be a whole lot better."

Chili's rival Applebee's launched a juicier chicken tender during its latest quarter, according to John Peyton, CEO of the chain's parent company Dine Brands. In 2027, Applebee's plans to overhaul more of its protein options.

"We think proteins are, number one, our biggest opportunity," Peyton told CNBC. "Number two, they're a big focus of the way people are thinking about eating right now, leaning into proteins and a little bit less with the carbs."

Chicken is also a focus for McDonald's, which is testing hand-breaded wings, strips and a sandwich in a handful of Chicago-area restaurants. It plans to expand the trial to more locations in the U.S. and Ireland in early 2027.

Moreover, McDonald's is trying to improve its coffee, with fresher beans and upgraded equipment, executives said at the company's investor day in September. Both chicken and coffee are fast-growing categories, offering more opportunities to lift sales than McDonald's signature beef burgers.

While the burger giant isn't in full turnaround mode like rivals Wendy's and Burger King, its U.S. business has struggled in recent quarters after its value strategy didn't gain the traction the company had hoped. In the third quarter, its U.S. same-store sales fell 0.2%.

In addition to menu tests, McDonald's is also leaning into training to improve the quality and consistency of its core menu items. Starting in October, the company launched "Make It Golden," a multiyear training program for corporate employees, restaurant workers and franchisees.

Ultimately, improved menu items mean little if the recipe changes aren't well executed.

#97. Home listings pile up in October, but sellers won't slash prices

Homebuyers continue to retreat, but sellers are still digging in, refusing to cut prices or give up. As a result, September's frozen housing market is now October's frozen and increasingly bloated market.

Active inventory in October hit 183,500 homes, up 7.4% year-over-year, according to Realtor.com. That is the fastest annual growth in eight months. Active listings have been rising each month since February. This is despite the fact that new listings are actually falling, down 7.5% from October 2025.

The market has seen several years of extremely lean supply, driven by the homebuying frenzy during the first two years of the pandemic, when mortgage rates hit more than a dozen record lows. Now supply nationally is just 6.6% below typical pre-pandemic levels, the narrowest gap since the pandemic.

"The market is cooling on both sides, but in different ways. Buyers are stepping back as higher mortgage rates cut into their buying power, while sellers are listing less often without a broad increase in delistings or price cuts," said Danielle Hale, chief economist at Realtor.com. "We're seeing a pause in new supply, but owners who already have homes on the market appear to be largely unphased."

Just over 20% of sellers cut prices in October, about the same as both September and October of last year. There was also no meaningful increase in delistings by sellers.

Mortgage rates came down a little bit in October month-to-month, but they are still over a full percentage point higher than a year ago. That continued to weigh on sales. Newly pending sales, representing signed contracts, not closings, dropped 16.8% in October year-over-year, according to Zillow. That was the biggest annual drop since August 2023 and slowest October since 2018.

While rates fell slightly, home prices continued to gain. Zillow estimated the monthly mortgage payment on the typical U.S. home was $2,004 in October, assuming a 20% down payment and excluding taxes and insurance. That is an increase of 12.8% from last year and 4.8% higher than in September, the biggest one-month jump in four years.

"November is shaping up to have an even larger contraction, but the silver lining is that next month is likely going to be the worst of it," said Mischa Fisher, Zillow's chief economist, in a release. "I expect the pace of the slowdown to ease into the new year. There are deals to be had for buyers able to brave the affordability headwinds in the coming months, and the continued inventory buildup means there is a lot of upside for sales into next year if we get rate relief."

CNBC's Property Play with Diana Olick covers new and evolving opportunities for the real estate investor, delivered weekly to your inbox.

#95. Eli Lilly’s Foundayo obesity pill eases sleep apnea in late-stage trial, paving way for approval

Eli Lilly on Monday said its obesity pill Foundayo significantly reduced the severity of obstructive sleep apnea, the most common sleep-related breathing disorder, in patients with obesity, in a late-stage trial.

The drugmaker plans to file by the end of the year for U.S. approval of the daily pill for moderate-to-severe obstructive sleep apnea, or OSA, in adults with obesity or who are overweight. The results make the pill — which rolled out in the U.S. in April — one of several GLP-1 treatments to show promise in treating the condition.

OSA refers to interrupted breathing during sleep due to narrowed or blocked airways, which can cause drops in oxygen and increases in blood pressure that are associated with serious complications, such as hypertension, heart failure and stroke. The condition affects nearly 1 billion adults globally, including 80 million people ages 20 and over in the U.S., according to the National Council on Aging. Most have never been diagnosed, Lilly said in a release.

Lilly's blockbuster weight loss injection Zepbound became the first medication to win U.S. approval for OSA in December 2024. Patients have limited treatment options outside of that drug and many use cumbersome machines that provide positive airway pressure, or PAP, to allow for normal breathing.

"Foundayo is already making a difference for people living with obesity, and with plans to submit these results to the FDA by the end of the year, we could have the opportunity to help people living with OSA," Dr. Thomas Seck, senior vice president of product development of Lilly Cardiometabolic Health, said in a release.

The trial tested Foundayo in two independent studies, the first of which included 363 patients who were unable or unwilling to use PAP therapy. The second study included 349 people who were on PAP therapy for at least three months at the time of screening and continued that treatment during the trial.

Patients in both studies who took Foundayo started at a low dose and increased it gradually at four-week intervals to different maximum dosages.

Researchers specifically examined how much the pill reduced the so-called apnea-hypopnea index, or AHI, which records the number of times per hour a person's breathing shows a restricted or completely blocked airway. The index is used to evaluate the severity of obstructive sleep apnea and the effectiveness of treatments for the condition.

People who took Foundayo and were not using PAP therapy had an average of 21.1 fewer AHI events per hour at 52 weeks, compared with an average reduction of 4.8 per hour for the placebo, according to the results.

Foundayo also met the trial's second goal in that group, leading to an overall AHI reduction of 43.8% compared with a decrease of 6.3% for the placebo. Lilly said 31.5% of the participants in the Foundayo group met the criteria for remission or improvement to mild OSA.

Meanwhile, patients who were on both Foundayo and PAP therapy had an average of 18.3 fewer AHI events per hour at 52 weeks, compared with an average reduction of 5.5 per hour for the placebo.

Foundayo led to an overall AHI reduction of around 27.5% in that group, compared with a decrease of 3% for the placebo. The company said 19.6% of the participants on Foundayo and PAP therapy also met the criteria for remission or mild OSA.

Foundayo also reduced what's known as the sleep apnea-specific hypoxic burden — a measure of how deeply and for how long oxygen levels fall during sleep. That metric fell by 55.9% in people not using PAP therapy and 45.8% in those using it.

Lilly said the pill also helped patients lose roughly 11% of their weight across groups at 52 weeks and helped to lower a marker of inflammation.

The company said the safety data and how well patients tolerated Foundayo were generally consistent with previous trials on the pill.

Around 12% of people on Foundayo who were not using PAP therapy discontinued due to side effects, compared to 3.8% with the placebo. Meanwhile, 6.8% of those on both Lilly's pill and PAP discontinued the drug due to side effects, compared with 0.6% in the placebo group.

The most common side effects were gastrointestinal, including nausea, diarrhea, constipation and vomiting, and were generally mild to moderate in severity. That's consistent with other GLP-1 medications.

#92. Nvidia's CEO turmoil gives newly empowered Democrats fresh ammunition in pursuit of oversight

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's close ties to the U.S. government were already poised to loosen with Democrats winning control of Congress and promising far more oversight of AI development than what the chip giant has experienced under Republican leadership.

That was before Huang admitted on Sunday to having an extramarital affair with a businesswoman who intelligence agencies say is a Chinese spy. The woman, Alice Huang, is affiliated with an Nvidia customer called Megaspeed that's been accused of helping restricted Chinese companies acquire Nvidia's artificial intelligence chips.

Huang apologized to staffers in a note on Sunday for being "unfaithful to my wife," and said the company's board was investigating any related national security concerns. Nvidia's board said it has "confidence in Jensen," who co-founded the company in 1993 and has led it ever since, building a net worth of more than $150 billion in the process.

Even before last week's midterm elections, which saw Democrats win sweeping majorities in the House and Senate, vocal party leaders wanted answers from Huang about China's access to advanced Nvidia chips and his company's lobbying for favorable legislation. Sen. Elizabeth Warren of Massachusetts said days before the election, when the spy story was first surfacing, that "we must pass bipartisan legislation to keep our advanced AI technology out of China's hands." Months before that, in June, Huang declined an invitation from Warren to testify before the Senate Banking Committee.

With Democrats about to take the gavel, Delaware Sen. Chris Coons, who is in line to chair the Senate Foreign Relations Committee, wrote on X on Saturday that "Congress will investigate and get answers," following the allegations that appeared in a New York Times report Friday.

It's a sharp reversal for Huang, who gained first-name status with President Donald Trump and has been closely allied with the administration in trying to limit AI regulation. Nvidia shares plunged 12% in the two days after the election, pacing a drop in AI-related stocks, on fears that incoming lawmakers, at the federal and state levels, and a host of newly passed ballot measures will slow the buildout and halt the AI boom.

Huang is a singular voice in the AI industry, as his company's technology is used to train nearly all major AI models. He's also turned Nvidia into an essential source of financing for AI startups, and pulled together some of the biggest names on Wall Street into a group that announced plans in August to raise $500 billion for investing in the buildout.

At Nvidia, Huang is the sole public face of the company and has no clear second-in-command. He decides which projects are a strategic priority, and frequently sends short emails to individual engineers about details. Huang has said he has 60 direct reports and reviews every employee's salary. Meanwhile, he packs his calendar with meetings with world leaders and CEOs, alongside keynote speeches at conferences and trade shows.

"Nvidia is not an ordinary company and Jensen is not an ordinary CEO; he IS Nvidia," Bernstein analyst Stacy Rasgon wrote in a note on Sunday, adding that it's not clear who would succeed him.

Patrick Moorhead, CEO at Moor Insights & Strategy, said in an email that "Jensen holding so much power is why Nvidia moves faster than anyone."

Unlike many of his tech peers, Huang is not chairman of the board. Rather, he's one of 11 board members, and there's no designated chair.

One investor close to Huang told CNBC that, depending on what the board's investigation finds, he could foresee Huang taking a leave of absence.

"Today's news will not slow Nvidia," a company spokesperson said in a statement. "Jensen is committed to continuing to lead Nvidia as CEO without interruption."

But scrutiny of Nvidia is set to intensify on Wall Street and in Washington, where Huang's level of influence is almost certain to change.

He has been a major advocate for more chip exports to China, arguing that it's better for AI labs there to build on American technology than to encourage the development of domestic alternatives. Huang joined Trump on Air Force One in May on a China trip that's now raised additional questions about what the administration and Huang knew about Chinese spying at the time.

One question Democrats are likely to probe is whether the Trump administration was aware of Huang's relationship with the alleged Chinese spy, and whether the Nvidia CEO influenced American policy, said Sam Bresnick, a research fellow at the Center for Security and Emerging Technology.

"That is going to be very high up on the Democrats' lists of people to investigate to try to figure out to what extent was the Trump administration aware of what was going on," Bresnick said.

But pressure isn't just coming from Democrats. House Republican leaders have already asked Huang for a closed-door briefing on Chinese spying issues later this month.

Trump has continued to back Huang, telling reporters on Sunday that he's an "honorable guy" and has "done an amazing job for our country."

"I think he probably has an excuse for it," Trump said of the incident.

Amid all the noise about the election results and the spying revelations, Nvidia is facing a crucial stretch on the markets.

Neoclouds CoreWeave and Nebius, which are both backed by Nvidia and are big buyers of its chips, are scheduled to report earnings this week after their stocks lost roughly half their value over the past month. Investors are particularly concerned about their hefty debt loads and how they'll continue to fund their buildouts with interest rates rising and lenders turning skittish.

Nvidia has its own earnings report coming on Nov. 17, when Huang, assuming he sticks with the typical format, will face questions from analysts. And then there's the Anthropic initial public offering, which is critical for Nvidia as it's both an investor in the Claude creator and counts the AI lab as a significant customer.

A couple of weeks after earnings, Nvidia is hosting a large conference in Washington, D.C., where Huang is scheduled to give the keynote on Dec. 1.

Bullish investors and analysts can still point to the insatiable demand for Nvidia's technology. The company recently projected 70% sales growth in its upcoming fiscal year. Analysts polled by FactSet expect nearly $694 billion in fiscal 2028 sales.

"We believe demand for the company's products seems unlikely to be affected," Rasgon wrote. "But it seems plausible to postulate increased risks (regulatory etc.) as AI buildouts become an increasingly political issue."

— CNBC's Seema Mody, Katie Tarasov, Ashley Capoot and MacKenzie Sigalos contributed reporting to this report.

#91. Goodyear Tire CEO Mark Stewart steps down as company continues turnaround plan

Goodyear Tire & Rubber Co. announced Monday that CEO Mark Stewart would be stepping down from his role effective immediately.

Board member Roger Wood is taking over as interim CEO and president, Goodyear said.

The leadership change comes as the tire company is in the midst of a multiyear transformation plan.

Stewart was hired in January 2024 to lead the company, with the goal of revitalizing the legacy American business to make it more efficient and profitable.

The company has faced disappointing results thus far, reporting last week third-quarter results that saw net sales down 2.3% year over year and an adjusted net loss of $31 million. Goodyear failed to reach a 10% operating margin by the end of last year, which was a major goal of the turnaround plan.

Goodyear said it formed a special committee of the board earlier this year to conduct a "strategic review of the company's businesses and operations focused on identifying opportunities to strengthen performance and drive shareholder value."

It said Monday it will continue that work by evaluating strategic and operational initiatives that include further optimizing the company's business mix and footprint, improving efficiency and investing in new products and capabilities that support long-term growth.

"Goodyear has made meaningful progress in becoming a more efficient and focused company, and the board believes this is the right moment to transition leadership to accelerate that progress and capture additional opportunity," Goodyear Chair Joe Hinrichs said in a press release.

The company has faced headwinds from tariffs, higher raw material costs and increased competition from China.

Wood has been a member of the company's board of directors since July 2023 and was previously the co-CEO of Tenneco as well as CEO of Dana Holding Corporation.

The company will start a search for a permanent replacement as part of its strategic review.

Shares of Goodyear were down roughly 4% in early trading Monday.
